And equally, of course, whenever we rebel against the sterile rigidity of formal thought and exposition and let our ideas run wild, we likewise lose. As I see it, the advances in scientific thought come from a combination of loose and strict thinking, and this combination is the most precious tool of science. (p. 75)

Bateson concluded that "rigor alone is paralytic death, but imagination alone is insanity."

— p. 130 —

The strategy of balancing these two processes, indeed, seems to be an important element of the genius of many of the great minds in history like Einstein and Edison. Certainly, it was the basis of many of Bateson's contributions. As he said, "We ought to accept and enjoy this dual nature of scientific thought and be willing to value the way in which the two processes work together to give us advances in understanding of the world." Unfortunately, the second (metaphorical) form of thinking is still not encouraged as part of scientific education today. In fact, it is considered 'unscientific' by many teachers. Even students of NLP often forget this important balance between the double descriptions of structure and intuitive thinking.

Exercise: "Living Metaphors"

The following exercise illustrates how Bateson's abductive thinking strategy may be applied to help solve problems.

1. Think of some problem you would like to deal with in a new way.

e.g., Inability to concentrate on studies.

Identify a specific physical location in front of you. Step into that location, associate into the problem situation and experience what is happening.

2. Step out of the problem situation to a Meta Position.

3. Think of something that you do that is completely unrelated to the problem situation and is a resource for you: i.e., an activity or ability that really gives you a sense of confidence, purpose, creativity, passion, etc.

e.g., Skiing.

Identify a different physical location in front of you for this resourceful activity or ability. Step into it and fully associate with the feeling of resourcefulness experience. Then start to loosely feel for the pattern which connects the resourceful activity or ability and the problem. Without thinking logically, explore in what ways they could be similar. They both share what?

e.g., They are both complex activities that involve the ability to direct attention properly.

4. Look over at the problem situation from the resource location. Make a metaphor for the problem situation in the context of the resource activity. Make it as visually clear as possible. That is, if the problem was being expressed in the context of the resource activity, what would you see happening?

e.g., Inability to concentrate is like constantly getting my skis crossed.

5. Find a genuine solution to the metaphoric representation of the problem in the context of the resource activity.

e.g., If you focus on controlling only one of the skis, the other one will follow it naturally.

6. Step back to Meta Position and determine how you would translate the metaphoric solution into the context of the actual problem situation. Then step into the problem situation to find if you have been able to break the impasse.

— p. 129 —

e.g., Focusing on controlling one of my skis is like clarifying my purpose and motivation for studying. If the 'why' is clear, the 'how' and the 'what' will follow naturally.

7. You may repeat the process with other resource activities in order to strengthen or deepen the solutions.

e.g., Gardening and studying both involve "cultivating."

Figure (p.129)

Metaphors Can Be Used as a Way to Transfer Resources from One Type of Activity or Situation to Another.

- Ideas are like flowers. The inability to concentrate on studies is like not properly watering or caring for certain seeds once they are in the ground because it will take time for them to grow and I do not know what they will blossom into.

- The solution is to place the seed package with a picture of the flower on a stake where the seeds have been planted so that I can see how beautiful it is going to be in the future and how much pleasure my effort will bring.

- Translating that to studying would be creating a list of the benefits I will receive from the subjects I need to study and visualizing myself attaining those benefits as the result of my studies.

Differences That Make a Difference

Just as Bateson balanced 'loose' and 'precise' thinking, and 'abductive' and 'deductive' logic he also complemented his search for 'the pattern which connects' by identifying the differences that makes a difference. Perception and cognition both require contrast. Bateson used claim that "it takes two to know one." We know 'hot' in relation to 'cold', 'light' in relation to 'dark', 'effort' in relation to 'rest', 'independence' in relation to 'dependence', etc. As Bateson put it, "Information is a difference that makes a difference." In fact, he went so far as to say that "the interaction between parts of mind is triggered by difference." He claimed:

...any ongoing ensemble of events and objects which has the appropriate complexity of causal circuits and the appropriate energy relations will surely show mental characteristics. It will

---

— p. 128 —
compare, that is, be responsive to difference... It will "process information" and will inevitably be self-corrective either toward homeostatic optima or toward the maximization of certain variables.

This is the essence of cybernetics. Cybernetic systems have a fundamental property of being "self-correcting." That is, they contain mechanisms that counteract influences that deflect the system from some optimum or desired state. A classic example of a cybernetic mechanism is that of a thermostat, which automatically controls the temperature in a room or building, keeping it from getting either too hot or too cold. If the space becomes too cold it turns the heater on. If the space becomes too hot it turns off the heater or turns on the air conditioner.

Cybernetic processes are essential for the proper functioning of any system, and the survival of the system is threatened when there is interference to these mechanisms. As Gregory Bateson explained:

All biological and evolving systems... consist of complex cybernetic networks, and all such systems share certain formal characteristics. Each system contains subsystems which are potentially regenerative, i.e., which would go into exponential "runaway" if uncorrected... The regenerative potentialities of such subsystems are typically kept in check by various sorts of governing loops to achieve "steady state."... A constancy of some variables is maintained by changing other variables... Those mutational changes will be perpetuated which contribute to the constancy of that complex variable we call "survival." The same logic also applies to learning, social change, etc.

Cybernetic descriptions and models are different from statistical or linear models in that they deal with the feedback of total systems — systems in which events at any position in the system may be expected to have effect at all positions on the system at later times. In cybernetic models, a particular cause or effect cannot be isolated from its context. Therefore, each part must be considered and measured in terms of the whole. Human behavior and experience are undoubtedly the result of such a system.

Cybernetics and the Importance of Feedback

The field of cybernetics developed as an interdisciplinary approach to the study of control and communication in animals, humans, machines, and organizations. Coined by U.S. mathematician Norbert Wiener (1894 – 1964) in 1946, the word "cybernetics" comes from the Greek term kybernetes, meaning "steersman." The basic premise of cybernetics is that "feedback" is the essential element for managing and directing the behavior of a system.

The original concept grew out of Wiener's and his colleague Julian Bigelow's work on automating control systems for aiming anti-aircraft guns during World War II by "feeding back" operating data into the system drawn from the interactions of the system with its environment. After the war, Wiener expanded his theories based on observations of interactions between humans, animals, and the physical environment. Wiener described the many ways in which organisms modify their own behavior to correct for adverse reactions to some other aspect of their behavior—i.e., in response to feedback.

---

— p. 127 —

Feedback refers to the process in which part of the output of a machine, organism or system is fed back—that is, returned—as input in order to guide or regulate its behavior. The returned part of the output is called "feedback."

Figure (p.127)

Feedback Comes from Returning Part of the System's Output as New Input

Feedback can also come from less direct sources than the behavioral output of the system. Feedback may be derived from the environmental consequences of the system's behavior or output, or as a result of an observer or monitor (such as teacher, camera or meter) of the system's output and its environmental consequences.

Other Sources of Feedback Include Environmental Consequences of Behavior and an Observer or Monitor

When the feedback signal reinforces a trend of the system, it is said to be "positive." When it opposes a trend of the system, it is called "negative" (or "corrective") feedback. Positive feedback is necessary for parts of a system to grow or proliferate. Negative feedback is employed to balance or stabilize parts of a system.

Negative Feedback Opposes Deviation and Encourages Stability

Positive Feedback Supports Deviation and Encourages Growth

---

— p. 126 —

Both positive and negative feedback are necessary for the effective development and operation of any system, biological or mechanical. They are also necessary to ensure a system's ability to fit into the other ecosystems to which it belongs.

Cybernetics and Ecology

Ecology has been defined as "the study of interrelationships and interdependence between organisms, and between organisms and their environments." The word "ecology" comes from the Greek, oikos, meaning "house," and logos, which means "word," "knowledge" or "principle," reflecting the emphasis of ecology on the fit of an organism with a particular 'habitat'. The term is generally believed to have been coined by German naturalist Ernst Haeckel, who first used and defined it in 1869. While the historical roots of ecology lie in biology and natural history, the principles of ecology are important for any field involving the interactions of elements which form complex systems (such as organizations, families, communities, or an individual's emotional and mental life). The notion of ecology and the "environmental consequences" of our actions was central to Bateson's thinking and his contributions. It is not surprising that he titled his seminal book Steps to an Ecology of Mind.

Inspired by Bateson, the NLP notion of "personal ecology" relates to the degree of fit or harmony of a person's actions with the rest of their interpersonal ecosystem. When people set goals and attempt to make changes, they often fail to consider the 'ecological' impact of those changes with respect to the various systems of which they are members. Thus, an "ecological outcome" is one which fits harmoniously with the rest of the individual's system(s).

In addition to involving many perspectives within a system, assessing ecology must also acknowledge the fact that a particular individual may be a member of many overlapping systems. Consider for a moment how many different types of systems you are a member of at this very moment. They may include systems related to family, environment, politics, economy, profession, culture, etc. (It has been calculated that the average person is a member of over 160 systems simultaneously.)

In addition to seeking the pattern which connects and the factors which created harmony in such ecosystems, Bateson also sought to discover and understand the patterns which created disconnection and disrupted harmony and ecology. One such potential pattern is that of schismogenesis.

Schismogenesis

The term schismogenesis means "that which creates or generates splits or separations." In systems theory, schismogenesis is a form of "deviation amplification", in which a system operates to increase, exaggerate or split off some particular characteristic or element. Deviation amplifying processes operate on the basis of "positive" feedback loops which produce increasing diversity and complexity (somewhat like the mechanism of a mathematical fractal). Schismogenesis is thus the amplification of difference. This is in contrast to deviation inhibiting processes, which emphasize "negative" feedback to stop variation and produce stability, homogeny and homeostasis.

---

— p. 125 —

In the study of evolutionary development, for instance, the term "schismogenesis" is used to describe the process by which a species divides or diverges into new forms in order to promote the adaptation of the species or group. In this case, differentiation or separation of species characteristics is necessary for the species to evolve and adapt to changing conditions or environments.

The term "schismogenesis" can also be applied to the creative process of many people. New ideas, principles and mental models of the world are often created as a result of processes which operate to separate them or differentiate them from others. The dialectical process of "thesis, antithesis and synthesis," for example, can be seen as the result of an initial process of schismogenesis (i.e., thesis versus antithesis), followed by the integrating process of synthesis.

If schismogenesis is not held in check by other stabilizing or corrective processes, however, it can produce potentially destructive polarization and conflict. In fact, one implication of the notion of schismogenesis is that it is something which "drives apart" other elements of a system. Thus, schismogenesis may be either adaptive or destructive depending on the presence of other systemic factors. Schismogenesis, for instance, becomes a problem when it is placed in an 'all or nothing' framework which prevents any eventual synthesis or integration.

Bateson used the term "schismogenesis" to describe the process of a growing pathological splitting up of a system. He found that certain ongoing interactions between elements of a system (as opposed to any particular event) frequently created a growing separation of elements that were critical for survival. If the separated elements became sufficiently out of balance, the system would break down—as in schizophrenia (which literally means "split-soul"). Bateson contended that schismogenesis was one of the primary causes of war and conflict between nations and cultures.

By better understanding the processes underlying schismogenesis, we can more effectively balance it and harness its positive evolutionary effects.

Cultural Schismogenesis and Its Causes

In Steps to an Ecology of Mind (1972, pp. 68-72), Gregory Bateson cites schismogenesis as one of the primary potential sources of conflict and disintegration in groups and cultures. According to Bateson, wars, "arms races" and "ethnic cleansings" are all the result of 'cultural schismogenesis'. As a part of his anthropological studies, Bateson identified the causes of cultural schismogenesis, and also some of the possible processes which can help to restrain it, and its potentially damaging effects.

In his study of cultures, Bateson identified two primary forms of processes which lead to separation and breakdown in the cultural system: symmetrical differentiation and complementary differentiation. Symmetrical schismogenesis occurs when the members of two groups each exhibit the same negative behavior toward each other, and the same positive behavior amongst themselves. In street gangs, for instance, gang members treat members of their own gang with respect and camaraderie but treat members of the opposite gang with anger and violence (this is commonly known as a "double standard"). A classic example of symmetrical behavior that can lead to problems is a shouting match or shoving match: one person

---

— p. 124 —

pushes another, so the second person pushes back a little harder. The first person in turn, pushes again even harder, and so on until a fight breaks out.

Complementary schismogenesis occurs when the reactions of two groups to each other are different but reinforce each other negatively. The generation gap of the sixties is a good example of complementary schismogenesis. The "radicals" had their own dress code and behavior patterns amongst one another (i.e., long hair, rock music, psychedelic drugs, etc.). The "establishment" had a different set of patterns and rules with which to interact with one another. Further, the militancy of the radicals and conservatism of the establishment were complementary to one another. The more unconforming the radicals were, the more conservative the establishment became, and vice versa. When there are no restraints on such a relationship it can grow into a harmful splitting. This is the type of splitting that Karl Marx claimed happened between capitalists and workers—the wealthy capitalists grew richer and richer, and the working class grew poorer and poorer. Similar schisms could occur between assertive and submissive or victim and rescuer behavior in a family system, and so on.

To summarize:

1. Symmetrical Differentiation:

Group 1 exhibits behavior A, B, C to each other and X, Y, Z to group 2. Group 2 exhibits behavior A, B, C to each other and behavior X, Y, Z to group 1. Certain types of behavior (e.g. boasting, hostility, accusation, etc.) can lead to unrestrained excess, such as the historical the arms race between the United States and the Soviet Union, or the terrorist activities between Northern and Southern Ireland in the 1970s and 80s.

Figure (p.124)

In Symmetrical Differentiation, the Actions of the Groups Reflect One Another

2. Complementary Differentiation:

Group 1 exhibits behavior L, M, N to each other and behavior O, P, Q to group 2. Group 2 exhibits behavior R, S, T to each other and behavior U, V, W to group 1. If one is assertive and the other submissive, this can lead to "progressive unilateral distortion" of the members of both groups, which can lead to hostility and unrestrained excess, as in the problems that can arise between different social classes and castes.

---

— p. 123 —

Figure (p.123)

In Complementary Differentiation, the Groups Respond Differently to Each Other

Restraints to Schismogenesis

Bateson proposed the principle of reciprocity as a way to constrain both forms of schismogenesis. A reciprocal relationship is neither symmetrical nor complementary. Behaviorally, a reciprocal relationship would be one in which both parties have equal abilities—say they are both able to be assertive and submissive—but they are able to respond to one another with the appropriate combination to create a balance. So, instead of spiraling into an escalating loop, it becomes a corrective loop.

Bateson pointed out that, instead of 'symmetry'—where group 1 responds with behavior X and group 2 responds with behavior X back—in a 'reciprocal' relationship, when group 1 responds with behavior X, group 2 responds with behavior Y, and vice versa. Sometimes group 2 follows and group 1 leads, for instance, and at other times group 1 follows and group 2 leads. Thus, "In every single instance the behavior is asymmetrical, but symmetry is regained over a large number of instances."

We can observe this type of reciprocity in high functioning musical groups and sports teams for instance when group members take turns being support roles or lead roles. As these examples suggest, however, effective reciprocity requires both awareness of the system by its members and feedback between the members of the system.

Another restraint to schismogenesis suggested by Bateson involved an admixture of complementary behavior in a symmetrical relationship, or an admixture of symmetrical behavior in a complementary relationship. This also creates a type of reciprocity. Co-founders of a venture taking the lead in different areas (e.g., design versus marketing) or collaborations where members contribute different strengths while maintaining equal standing would be examples of an admixture of complementary behavior in a symmetrical relationship. Parents letting children "be in charge" of some task or event, or a coach letting a team member "call a play," would be examples of bringing symmetrical behavior into a complementary relationship.

According to Bateson, the effectiveness of these types of reciprocity was influenced by several other factors:

1. Meta Awareness of the relationship—where both groups are aware of the nature of their relationship. In this case, both groups would be able to take a "meta position" with respect to their relationship with the other group. The ability of two conflicting parties to reflect on the longer-term patterns and history of their

---

— p. 122 —

interaction as part of the process of reaching a solution during a negotiation would be an example of this.

2. Being united by some larger common goal outside of the relationship. A natural disaster, for example will often motivate conflicting groups to work together toward the common goal of survival.

3. Symbiosis — in particular where the types of behavior in a complementary relationship are mutually beneficial. In other words, managers and employees, teachers and students, parents and children, etc., each benefit from the relationship (i.e., it is "win-win").

Bateson's understanding of the causes of schismogenesis, and, in particular, its restraints is an example of a key pattern of genius: engaging multiple perspectives and entertaining different points of view. This was one of Bateson's strengths. In fact, he claimed "wisdom comes from being able to sit together and truthfully confront our differences, without the intent to change them." By holding multiple maps of a particular phenomenon we get a deeper, more holistic understanding. Just like his ability to engage both loose and rigorous thinking, Bateson emphasized the importance of what he called "multiple-description" that integrated different perspectives as the approach to think about or reflect upon a particular experience or phenomenon.

Application of Bateson's Restraints to Schismogenesis to Conflict Resolution in NLP

Like all systemic truths, Bateson's restraints to schismogenesis with respect to cultural conflict apply equally to organizations, groups and individuals.

They are directly applied in the NLP approach to resolving conflicts, which can be summarized in the following steps.

1. Clearly identify the key issues involved in the conflict. These issues will be expressed as either opposites or polarities.

e.g., investing or spending money versus saving money

2. Establish an unbiased 'meta-position' that is clearly distinct from either of the parts or parties in conflict and bring awareness to the relationship. It can be useful at this step to determine what type of schismogenesis is creating or contributing to the conflict. Is it symmetrical or complementary?

e.g., spending money versus saving money = complementary schismogenesis

3. Find the positive intention and purpose behind the issues of each part or party. The positive intention will necessarily be at a higher level than the issues creating the conflict. Positive intentions will typically not be opposites or polarities. More often they are symbiotic and beneficial systemically as opposed to individually.

e.g., spending money = "growth"; saving money = "security"

4. Make sure that each part or party recognizes and acknowledges the positive intent of the other. This does not mean that either one has to accept the method with which the other is attempting to satisfy the positive intention, nor does it mean that either part or party has to compromise his or her position.

— p. 121 —

5. From meta position keep 'chunking up' until a common intention on a higher level has been identified that both parts or parties share. What is a larger common goal or purpose that both sides want or serve? This can be done by asking, "What is the purpose of having growth/security?"

e.g., sustainable evolution

6. Explore other alternatives for achieving the shared intention than the two current choices which are producing the conflict. This may include a mixture of the two existing choices but should include at least one alternative that is completely distinct from the two in conflict.

e.g., invest some money and save some money, borrow money, create an alternative income stream, find an investment partner, downsize some expenditures so that money can be invested in other areas, etc.

7. Identify which choice or combination of choices will most effectively and ecologically satisfy the common intention and the individual positive intentions with the greatest positive impact systemically. What is the feedback that will indicate which choice or combination to use in a particular situation?

It is evident that these steps integrate all of Bateson's restraints to schismogenesis: meta awareness of the relationship, unification with respect to a common larger goal, symbiosis and reciprocity.

There are some conflicts, however, that require a deeper level of awareness and understanding in order to find a resolution. Bateson called these "double binds."

Bateson and the "Double Bind"

A double bind is a special type of conflict that creates a "no-win" situation; i.e., a situation in which "you are damned if you do, and damned if you don't." According to Bateson, such conflicts are at the root of both creativity and psychosis. What makes the difference is whether or not one is able to identify and transcend the bind in an appropriate way.

Figure (p.121)

Choice, Conflict and Double Binds

— p. 120 —

Both standard conflicts and double binds arise from situations in which a person, group or organization must choose a course of action. In situations involving free choice, the actor selects between more or less equal alternatives according to a particular criterion – i.e., choosing which type of soap to buy at the supermarket, or which television channel to watch. Free choice is almost completely "internally referenced" or "self-referenced," meaning that the selection is made totally on the basis of its fit with the actor's unique criteria or wishes:

A is fun. B is more fun. C is profitable. D is both fun and profitable, etc.

More challenging decision-making situations arise when an actor has external constraints to contend with as well as internal criteria – i.e., there is only a certain amount of money, time, etc., which limits the choices the actor can make. So, the choice the actor makes begins to become more "externally referenced." Decision making, in this case, involves selecting the 'best' alternative for action based on both the internal criteria and external constraints the actor is dealing with.

Conflicts emerge when there are multiple criteria and constraints that lead to courses of action that are to some degree mutually exclusive.

A is fun but we must do B instead because it is safer.

According to Bateson, the issues related to conflict situations are usually stated as injunctions related to some course of action. These injunctions may be stated either positively (i.e., toward something, as in the example above), or negatively (i.e., away from something). Negative injunctions would have the form:

"We must not do A because it is dangerous."

Negative injunctions bring our attention to what is to be avoided. So instead of focusing on what we want, all of our attention is directed to what we don't want.

Double binds have another feature that makes them even more difficult in that any course of action will lead to something to be avoided. Consider the following humorous double bind formulated by the comedian Woody Allen (from Side Effects, 1980):

More than any other time in history, mankind faces a crossroads. One path leads to despair and utter hopelessness. The other to total extinction. Let us pray that we have the wisdom to choose correctly.

When a person finds himself or herself in such a double bind, he or she is generally no longer attempting to find the best way to achieve some desired goal or criterion but is rather devoting all his or her energy to avoiding pain, punishment, annihilation, etc. Instead of focusing on what s/he wants, the person is forced to choose between "the lesser of two evils."

Double binds become even more intense when they relate to a single course of action. Bateson illustrated this type of double bind using the example of the "bread-and-butterflies," from the children's story Alice in Wonderland. The "bread-and-butterflies" were creatures whose wings were made of thin slices of bread-and-butter, and whose head was a lump of sugar. Upon seeing one for the first time, Alice asks, "And what does it live on?" She was told that they feed upon "weak tea with cream in it." In considering this, Alice notices a dilemma. The tea would dissolve the lump of sugar, and thus the creature's

— p. 119 —

head, and it would die. "Supposing it couldn't find any tea?" she then asks. "Then it would die, of course." Reflecting on the creature's predicament, Alice mused that the creature must quite often have trouble surviving. "It always does," she is told.

The bread-and-butterfly is in a classic double bind. If it does not find any tea to drink, it will die of starvation. But if it does find tea and drink it, the tea will dissolve its head, and it will die. Thus, the essential structure of this type of double bind is:

If we do A we will not (survive, be safe, have fun, etc.) but if we do not do A we will not (survive, be safe, have fun, etc.).

In both the Woody Allen example and the "bread-and-butterflies" allegory the double bind is created by external circumstances. According to Bateson, though, the more severe double binds capable of creating significant mental distress were those created interpersonally – where the actions of one person or group put another person or group into a "no win" situation.

Bateson's Ingredients for an Interpersonal Double Bind

In his book Steps to an Ecology of Mind (1972, pp. 206-208), Gregory Bateson summarizes the essential 'ingredients' for an interpersonal double bind in the following way:

1. The individual involved is in an intense relationship in which s/he feels that it is vitally important to discriminate accurately what sort of message is being communicated, so s/he can respond appropriately.

The types of relationships at the root of an interpersonal double bind are typically "complementary" relationships, in which one person is in a "position of power," i.e., parent-child, boss-subordinate, teacher-student, etc. In such a relationship, the "dependent" individual cannot survive or get what he or she needs (support, acknowledgement, love, etc.) without the participation or cooperation of the other person. The relationship is thus "other oriented." The individual needs to please or respond appropriately to the other in order to get some fundamental need met.

There is also an implication that the person in the power position is "better than" or "more capable than" the other, because the dependent one is "deficient."

2. The person in the "position of power" is expressing two orders of message and one denies the other.

a. There is a primary negative injunction. For example, (1) "Do not do X or I will punish you." Or (2) "If you do not do X I will punish you." The punishment need not be physical, it may be disapproval, or abandonment because of helplessness. Thus, the double bound individual is constantly avoiding punishment rather than seeking reward.

b. There is also a secondary injunction conflicting with the first at a more abstract level, and like the first, enforced by punishments or signals that threaten survival or some other fundamental need.

1) This second message is commonly communicated nonverbally.

— p. 118 —

2) It may impinge upon any element of the primary injunction. For example, (a) "This is not punishment." (b) "I am not the punisher." (c) "Do not submit to me." (d) "Do not think of what you must not do." (e) "Do not question (me/my love/my intent)."

3. The 'dependent' individual is unable to comment on the messages being expressed in order to correct his or her discrimination of what order of message to respond to; i.e., he or she cannot make a meta-communicative statement. This may be either because the individual is confused or lacks the sophistication to recognize that he or she is in a double bind situation, or because meta-communication is not allowed or is dangerous.

4. There is a "tertiary negative injunction" prohibiting escape from the situation, because of survival, loyalty, love, punishment, etc. The "victim", and sometimes the "perpetrator", of the double bind are unable to "leave the field," trapping them in the situation, and preventing them from getting any distance or safety with respect to the situation.

5. It is a repeated experience, not a single traumatic event, so that the double bind becomes a habitual expectation.

As an illustration of these conditions, Bateson cites an example of a boy who had been hospitalized with the diagnosis of "schizophrenia." After some time in the hospital, the boy had become stable enough that he was able to receive visitors. One day, the boy's mother came to visit. Upon greeting her son, the mother said, "How about giving your mother a hug?" The boy dutifully put his arms around her. As he embraced her, however, the

boy's mother visibly stiffened, seeming uncomfortable with the physical contact. Responding to the non-verbal message, the boy withdrew his arms, somewhat confused. At this point, the mother asked, "What's wrong, don't you love your mother?" Becoming even more confused and uncomfortable, the boy started to tense up and look away. At this, his mother admonished, "You really must learn to control your emotions." The interaction continued in this way until the boy's anxiety escalated, ending in a violent episode for which he had to be physically restrained.

The relationship between the mother and the boy is clearly complementary. In addition to being his parent, the boy needs his mother's approval and cooperation to be released from the hospital. The primary negative injunction is, "If you do not hug me, you do not love me (and thus will not have my approval)." The secondary injunction, communicated non-verbally, is, "If you do hug me, you will make me uncomfortable, and I will withdraw (and thus will not have my approval)." The boy cannot leave the situation voluntarily and is unable to comment on the bind he is experiencing.

There is also a third message, however, which seems to be significant. This message is a communication on the part of the mother about the boy's reaction to his dilemma. Her comment that, "You really must learn to control your emotions," implies that the source of the problem is with the boy's inability to control his emotions rather than in her own incongruity. The implication is, "The fact that you feel confused means that something is wrong with you. You are the cause of the problem, confusion, etc."

— p. 117 —

This third message seems to be an important part of the interpersonal double bind pattern. The person in the "power position" interprets the dependent individual's discomfort or confusion as a sign of (1) incompetence, or (2) negative intent on the part of the dependent individual. The third message is also typically an identity level. The implication being that the dependent individual's sense of confusion is evidence that he or she is defective at the level of his or her identity or, "What is wrong with this relationship resides within you." "You don't care about me." "You are messing with me." "You should know better than that."

Consider the example, also provided by Bateson, from the children's book Mary Poppins. In the book, the Banks children and Mary Poppins (who is their nanny) visit a cookie store. Upon entering the shop, they notice a couple of nervous looking girls behind the counter. A few moments later, the girls' mother comes from the back of the store and asks the Banks children if her daughters have offered them a gingerbread cookie yet. "No Mother, we were just going to," one of the girls responds. The mother then says in a "soft, fierce, terrifying" voice, "Just going to? Oh, indeed! That is very interesting. And who, may I ask, gave you permission to give away my gingerbread——?" The girl begins to reply, "Nobody, Mother. And I didn't give it away. I only thought——." "You only thought! That is very kind of you. But I will thank you not to think. I can do all the thinking that is necessary here!" says the mother, again "in her soft, terrible voice." As the child becomes visibly distressed and begins to cry, the mother laughs, points at the girl and says, "Look at her! Just look at her! Cowardy-custard! Cry-baby!"

Again, there are three messages: 1) if you don't offer the others a cookie, you have done something wrong and worthy of a reprimand, 2) if you do offer them a cookie, you have done something wrong and worthy of a reprimand, 3) your distress about being in a double bind is a sign of a defect in your character. It is this third aspect of the double bind that makes them so emotionally intolerable.

Bateson claimed that living in a family system or social context where one is constantly subjected to double binds such as these can create a great deal of emotional stress and contribute to certain types of psychological problems and even mental illness. He posited that such a situation went beyond schismogenesis and could become "schizophrenogenic." Bateson's suggestion that the double bind is a possible factor in the development of schizophrenia and psychosis, has come under considerable criticism since its proposal. Many psychiatrists view the causes of schizophrenia to be essentially of physiological and genetic origin. It is also claimed that Bateson's theory puts too much blame on the family, and in particular the mother, of the schizophrenic patient.

Whatever the merits of these criticisms, however, it is clear that interpersonal double binds can create intense emotional and psychological stress

Resolving Double Binds

While double binds certainly appear formidable, it is possible to get out of them, given the appropriate level of awareness and skill. Bateson's comment in his preface to Bandler and Grinder's first book that "making explicit the syntax of how people avoid change" we can know better "how to assist them in changing" is a description one of his own meta strategies of genius. This is

— p. 116 —

a very powerful strategy. By understanding the structure of what creates a problem we may be able to deduce a solution. This has also been a strategy applied many times in NLP. By understanding how phobias are created, for example, Bandler and Grinder were able to create the celebrated NLP 10-minute phobia technique (which has proven an effective method to relieve many people of unnecessary fears over the past 50 years).

While Bateson himself never proposed any explicit therapeutic steps for resolving interpersonal double binds, getting relief from a double bind situation essentially comes from dealing with the "conditions" which are required to produce the double bind. This would involve developing the ability to:

1. Reduce the intensity or shift the nature of the double binding relationship. 2. Sort out the contradictory messages. 3. Make metacommunicative statements. 4. Find a way to "leave the field". 5. Keep the situation from being a repeated experience.

Exploring how this could be done can also give us deeper insight into Bateson's genius.

1. Reducing the intensity and dependency of the relationship.

Reducing the intensity of a 'double binding' relationship involves establishing physical and emotional independence, by developing greater self-awareness, a stronger internal self-reference and acquiring a clearer sense of one's own "first position" perspective. A person can also reduce the degree of "complementarity" in the relationship, diminishing the "power" held by the other person, by becoming less dependent on the other person. Some people literally "outgrow" a particular double bind situation.

Key to reducing dependency is to generate other options and choices. Bateson maintained that, frequently, we are limited not so much by external reality but by our internal map and assumptions about a situation. Resolving a double bind requires getting out of the "box" created by those mental maps and assumptions. This is why Bateson contended that double binds are frequently a powerful source of creativity and even genius.

As an example, Bateson cites a common feature of Zen training. A Zen master picks up a stick and raises it over one of his students, saying, "If you say that this stick is real, I will hit you with it. If you say that this stick is not real, I will hit you with it. Is this stick real or not?" As long as the student's focus stays within the narrow "box" of possibilities established by the words the master has used to create the double bind, he or she is stuck. If the student simply reaches up and grabs the stick from the Zen master, begins to sing, picks up his or her own stick and pretends to "sword fight," etc., he or she has transcended the double bind, and shifted the context of the relationship and the situation.

Perception of context seems to be an important component of double binds (and thus breaking out of them). A double bind presupposes some kind of tacit understanding in which it is assumed that, "in this situation, these are the only options." A key part of being able to break out of double binds involves the ability to recognize and transcend such presuppositions.

— p. 115 —

In the case of the mother in the cookie store, for example, Bateson wonders, why don't her girls simply look knowingly at one another and say, "There goes Mom again. She sure acts grouchy sometimes." They could also have said to their mother, "Look Mom, are we supposed to give them the cookies or not? Be clear please." Somehow both the children and the mother are trapped by their inner map and assumptions about each other's intentions, the possible responses and the consequences of those responses.

A fundamental principle of change in NLP is that "behind every behavior there is, at some level, a positive intention or purpose." According to NLP, once the positive intention is discovered, other choices of behavior can be found which still satisfy the intention but lead to different, more ecological consequences.

This principle is often particularly challenging for people to apply with respect to the "perpetrators" of double binds. What could possibly be the positive intention or purpose behind putting someone into a double bind? What positive reason would someone have to "make someone else wrong?" On the surface, it would seem that the intention of the perpetrator might be to maintain power or control. Other intentions (if the perpetrator is actually conscious of creating a double bind) might include to indoctrinate others, draw them into the same bind as the perpetrator (misery loves company), or to prepare the "victim" for a worse situation (a type of immunization for other, more challenging double binds). The positive aspects of these various intentions may be summarized as to "be strong" or "belong."

Again, from an NLP perspective, the recognition of the intention behind the double bind opens up the possibility of helping both the "perpetrator" and "victims" to find other choices of behavior which satisfy the positive aspects of the intent, but which circumvent the pathologies of the double bind.

2. Sorting out contradictory messages.

A person can learn to sort out contradictory messages by becoming more aware of incongruencies in the communications of others, especially incongruence involving non-verbal 'meta messages' (such as when a person says "yes" but is shaking his or her head "no"). Often, people find themselves feeling confused or trapped simply because they are unable to detect such double messages when they receive them.

One interesting way to learn to recognize contradictory messages is to actually practice sending them. For example, send a verbal injunction "Do X," e.g., say "Come closer," and simultaneously send the contradictory non-verbal injunction, "Do not do X," e.g., Hold up hands indicating, "Keep away." Or say, "Comfort me" in a threatening tone of voice. Doing this type of exercise also gives and interesting insight into the internal experience behind sending such messages.

As Bateson pointed out, "Words are dangerous things." Learning to recognize potential double binds in verbal statements is an important step in being able to deal with them more constructively. Consider, for example, the following statements:

You are wrong the way you are, but you can't change.

— p. 114 —

I am doing this to teach you a lesson, even though you will never learn.

I'm unhappy with how things are, but they will only get worse if you try to change anything.

Being able to recognize these types of conflicting messages is an important part of resolving double binds. The way that such statements are structured tend to limit a person's perspective, blinding him or her to choices that would be obvious if the options were presented differently. In addition to the Meta Model pattern known as modal operators of necessity (should, must, have to, etc.), these statements also include Meta Model patterns relating to cause-effect (X causes A), complex equivalence (Y means B), and universal quantifiers (always, never, every, any, etc.).

The identification of these language patterns was one of the reasons that Bateson was so impressed with Bandler and Grinder's book The Structure of Magic.

3. Making metacommunicative statements.

Recognizing contradictory messages can lead naturally to making metacommunicative statements. By simply commenting on the incongruence one is observing, the contradictory "injunctions" one is receiving, and the corresponding confusion one is feeling, one is already naturally "metacommunicating"—i.e., communicating about the communication one is receiving, or the double bind one is experiencing.

Thus, it is important that the metacommunication about a double bind not be an accusation, but rather an honest appraisal of one's experience. In this sense, metacommunication requires the ability to take a meta position.

A basic structure with to which make a metacommunicative statement about a double bind would be:

"I am in a double bind about X."

"If I don't do X, I feel I am 'wrong' because _________________."

"If I do do X, I feel I am 'wrong' because _________________."

As an example, had the hospitalized boy in Bateson's illustration been able to metacommunicate, he may have said something like, "Mother, I am in a double bind about hugging you. If I don't hug you, I feel I am wrong because you will (say/think) I don't love you. If I do hug you, I feel I am wrong because I feel you stiffen up and it seems as if I am making you feel embarrassed and uncomfortable."

As another example, the girls in the cookie store in the Mary Poppins story might have been able to metacommunicate by saying, "Mother, we are in a double bind about giving the Banks children a cookie. If we don't offer them a cookie, and wait to ask you first, we feel we are wrong because your voice tone implies we have been impolite. If we do offer them a cookie without waiting to ask, we feel that we are wrong because your angry voice implies we have been disobedient."

Obviously, making such a metacommunicative statement involves the ability to not only sort the contradictory messages but also to be able to take

— p. 113 —

a meta position with respect to the relationship. It also requires a strong sense of self-awareness and self-worth along with the courage to speak up. This is why finding ways to do the first step of reducing the intensity and dependency of the relationship is so important.

A potentially important consequence of making metacommunicative statements is that they can make the "perpetrator" of the double bind more aware of his or her own incongruent messages. People who create double binds for others are often not aware of their own conflicting injunctions. Sometimes, being confronted with their own double messages begins a process of self-awareness and change within the person who is sending them.

4. Finding a way to "leave the field".

One of the most frustrating and challenging things about being in an interpersonal double bind comes from the "tertiary negative injunction" prohibiting escape from the situation. Even if one is not able to resolve the rest of the double bind situation, being able to "leave the field" can often provide enough relief to prevent the situation from becoming intolerable. Having "somewhere else to go" can help a great deal in relieving the sense of desperation associated with double binds.

Sometimes, people discover that nothing really prevents them from physically escaping the situation. Instead, they are trapped by their own beliefs, or the limits of their "map of the world." By simply being reminded that they can leave, or have other choices, people can often get out of the most excruciating aspects of a double bind situation.

Another way to "leave the field" is by going to an "observer position" or "third position" with respect to the relationship. In NLP, a third position would involve the ability to be outside of, or above oneself, looking down not only on one's own actions, but also one's interactions with others; as if one were viewing a movie or video. This allows a person the chance to go to a different perspective or find a context that is impervious to the ongoing double bind situation. Going to third position makes it possible to transform the situation by "widening the field of possibilities."

This shift in perspective is also facilitated by using third person language (he, she, they, them, etc.) when describing yourself and any others involved in the interaction. The process is the key step in the NLP procedures for addressing phobias, traumas and pain. Usually, the emotional intensity associated with the situation is immediately reduced if one can accomplish this shift in perceptual position.

It is also possible to take multiple observer positions; i.e., by watching yourself watch yourself or event watching yourself, watch yourself, watching yourself. If necessary, the process could be extended even further (there is an unlimited number of potential observer positions). Each new observer position not only distances you further from the emotional intensity of the situation but also allows you to take a broader and potentially wiser perspective.

5. Keeping the situation from being a repeated experience.

Probably the best way to keep a double bind situation from becoming a repeated experience is to develop and apply the capabilities that help to diffuse or transform double binds. When a person is confident that he or she

— p. 112 —

knows how to address or resolve a particular double bind situation, the fact that it has occurred more than once in the past is no longer as much of a problem. In other words, once a person learns how to do something or solve a problem, the fact that he or she has not been able to do it before is of less relevance.

As Bateson contended, interpersonal double binds are a challenging but common phenomenon that can be either the source of insight and creativity, or of stress and even severe psychological symptoms, depending on the type of bind and the resources one is able to mobilize in order to address it. By understanding the deeper structure of pathological double binds, it is possible to develop skills and strategies for transcending or transforming them.

Another way to keep a repeated double bind "situation" from being a repeated "experience" is to develop strategies to compartmentalize or isolate double bind situations — that is, distinguishing between double bind situations as "events" versus perceiving them as a generalization about one's fate or "identity." An "event" is a behavior that occurs in a particular environment; what in a particular where and when. To perceive something as a repeated experience, or "life pattern," would involve generalizing it to the level of belief and identity: to who and why. People who have experienced double bind situations in their lives, but who have been able to remain relatively unaffected by them, will perceive them as "something that happened to me along with other challenging events in my life," rather than as "something that has shaped who I am." Accomplishing this involves an awareness and understanding of different "logical levels" of experience which I will cover in the next section.

Bateson's Notions of Logical Types and Logical Levels

One of Bateson's most significant and powerful contributions to the natural and social sciences was that of "logical types" and "logical levels"—which he called the "most important criterion of mind" in his book Mind and Nature (1979). The notions of logical levels and logical types relate to the fact that any system of interaction is also a subsystem embedded inside another system, which is embedded inside another system, and so on. This kind of relationship between systems produces a type of "holarchy" of different levels of interaction and structure as depicted in the following illustration from Roots of NLP.

Figure (p.112)

— p. 111 —

Our Universe Is Made Up of an Ecology of Systems That Are Themselves Sub-Systems in Successively Larger Systems

Our brain structure, language, and social systems form natural hierarchies or levels of structures and processes. And the dynamics at one level of the holarchy are typically different from those at another level. Ideas, for instance, operate at a different level and according to different dynamics than the neurons in the brain which represent those ideas. As Bateson pointed out, "When we think of coconuts or pigs, there are no coconuts or pigs in the brain." The characteristics of a brain are not the same as a brain cell. The individual brain cell cannot "think" like the brain can. The two can, however, affect each other through indirect feedback—i.e., the functioning and connections of the overall brain can influence the behavior of a single brain cell, and the activity of a single brain cell contributes to the overall functioning of the brain. Indeed, a cell may be said to affect itself through the rest of the brain structure.

Figure (p.111)

An Idea Is a Different Level than the Particular Neurons in the Brain which Represent that Idea

This is one of the key foundations of Bateson's systemic thinking strategy and his genius – the notion that our minds, our bodies, our societies and our universe form an ecology of complex systems and sub-systems all of which interact with and mutually influence each other. It is not possible to completely isolate any part of the system from the rest of the system.

As an analogy, the 26 letters of the English alphabet can exist without the word "home," but the word "home" cannot exist without the alphabet. The word "home," however, is at a higher level than the letters of the alphabet because "home" organizes the letters of the alphabet and signifies or points to more than the alphabet itself. Similarly, sentences are at a higher level than words; paragraphs are at a higher level than sentences; chapters are a higher level than paragraphs; and so on. Each higher-level forms a greater whole because it includes yet is something more than the previous structures from which it is formed.

Each level is made up of relationships between parts of the level below it yet transcends those parts to make a more encompassing structure. For example, when two hydrogen atoms and an oxygen atom unite, the water made from their relationship is no longer just a mixture of individual hydrogen and oxygen atoms but is rather its own new entity, a different "logical type." This new entity has completely different properties than either hydrogen or oxygen in isolation. Yet, if we take away the hydrogen only oxygen remains. If we take away the oxygen, only hydrogen remains. So, what and where is the water? The water is the relationship between the atoms.

— p. 110 —

Thus, levels are created by relationships between other relationships. Making the relationship between two variables at a particular level a variable for a second relationship creates another level. This new level has its own unique qualities and characteristics and, therefore, becomes a different logical type from the lower levels.

We can say, then, that logical levels are operational – i.e., the higher level emerges from and operates upon relationships on the lower level. Logical types are structural – i.e., the higher level has a different structure and exhibits completely different qualities and characteristics from the lower level. And the two are completely interdependent. One cannot exist without the other.

In other words, logical types are hierarchies related to classification based on a class-member hierarchy of sets. Logical levels are hierarchies related to functions in which operations on one level select and organize elements on the level below it.

Consider the rate of change, or "speed" of an automobile. Speed is a function of how much ground the vehicle covers in a certain amount of time (e.g., 10 miles per hour). Thus, speed is the relationship between distance and time. The car's velocity in moving from the garage to the road can be said to be at a different level than a car, garage, road, or clock, because it is a property of the relationship between them (and does not exist without them).

The accelerator mechanism of the car is another level, as it creates changes in the vehicle's velocity. The person who is driving the car makes change in its acceleration and is at yet another level. The person's perception of the urgency with regard to getting to their destination is another level, etc.

We can see a similar dynamic in the relationship between letters, words, sentences, paragraphs, chapters and books. Each is a different level created by relationships between elements of the previous level. Each level is also a different type that has its unique characteristics. You can misspell a word, for example, but cannot "misspell" a letter or a book. So, what applies to one level or type cannot be applied to the others.

As another illustration, we use the relationship between light and dark markings on a screen or piece of paper to make an "image"; when we compare two images to find differences, we have jumped to the next logical level of process. Consider the following images.

Figure (p.110)

At one level there are different shades of light and dark blocks. At another level (if we squint our eyes or look at the images from a distance), we synthesize those blocks into an image of a face. At another level, we compare

— p. 109 —

the images and may conclude that they are of the same person with the head turned to a different angle. At yet another level we may recognize the face as being the American President, Abraham Lincoln.

Similarly, perceiving the markings below as being "men with hats and briefcases walking beside a building" is one level of process. Comparing the heights of the men is another type or level of process. [It should be noted that the men are actually all the exact same height. Their relationship to their environment, and our assumptions about foreshortening due to perceived distance, makes them appear to be different heights.]

Figure (p.109)

Bateson's Application of Logical Types and Logical Levels to Behavior

Bateson applied the notions of logical types and logical levels to many aspects of behavior and to biology. He pointed out that in the processes of learning, change and communication there are natural hierarchies of classification of information and knowledge. The function of each level in the hierarchy was to organize the information on the level below it, and the rules for changing something on one level of the hierarchy were different from those for changing at a different level. Changing something on a lower level could, but would not necessarily, affect the upper levels; but changing something in the upper levels would necessarily impact things on the lower levels in order to support the higher-level change.

In science and mathematics, for instance, hierarchy is used to denote "a series of ordered groupings of people or things within a system." Usually these groupings have "few things, or one thing, at the top and with several things below each other thing," like an inverted tree structure.

Hierarchies are often represented as "tree structures".

To Bateson, logical typing was a "law of nature," not simply an intellectual theory. In his view, our brain structure, language, and social systems form natural hierarchies or levels of processes of these types. Bateson believed

— p. 108 —

that many problems and pathologies emerged as a result of the confusion of logical types and levels and the failure to perceive and respect the discontinuities between them.

As a student of his Ecology of Mind class, Bateson instilled in me the importance of considering logical types and levels in all aspects of life and experience. And, because I was exposed to these ideas at the same time I was becoming involved in NLP, Bateson's approach has always been an integral part of my understanding of NLP. His distinctions about different logical types and levels of learning seemed of particularly profound significance.

I vividly remember discussing the power of Bateson's notions of logical levels and logical types with a participant in an NLP course that I was teaching in Oslo, Norway in 1986. The person was also familiar with Bateson's work, and we were reflecting on the deep importance of logical types and levels of learning. We both agreed, however, that these ideas had not been applied as fully and pragmatically as they could be. I recall saying, "Yes, someone really should apply the notion of logical levels in a more practical everyday sense." As soon as the words left my mouth, it was as if I had given myself the command, which led to my development of the NeuroLogical Levels of Change model and applications.

The Origins of Bateson's Model

Bateson derived the notion of different logical types of communication and learning from Bertrand Russell's mathematical theory of logical types—which states that a class of things cannot be a member of itself. According to Bateson (Steps to an Ecology of Mind, p.202):

Our approach is based on that part of communications theory which [Bertrand] Russell has called the Theory of Logical Types. The central thesis of this theory is that there is a discontinuity between the class and its members. The class cannot be a member of itself nor can one of the members be the class, since the term used for the class is of a different level of abstraction—a different Logical Type—from terms used for members.

As an example, the class of even numbers cannot itself also be an even number. Similarly, the class of cats is not a particular cat. Likewise, the physical object "cat" cannot be treated the same as the entire class of cats. (The class of cats does not require milk and kitty litter, but the members of the class frequently do.) In other words, the notion of logical types distinguishes between a particular "map" and the "territory" to which the map relates; i.e., between a mental "form" and its "content."

Bateson's classification of logical types was also partially influenced by General Semantics founder Alfred Korzybski who emphasized the importance of differentiating between various "orders of abstraction." These include differences between what we see (our internal representations) and the external stimuli themselves, and between verbal maps and the inner representations they intend to depict. Inner representations are more abstract but more encompassing than the external reality they represent. Similarly, verbal descriptions are more abstract and potentially more encompassing than the inner representations they stand for. In fact, it was Korzybski who first said, "The map is not the Territory."

— p. 107 —

According to Korzybski, differentiating orders of abstraction also included distinguishing between (a) descriptions of experiences and (b) inferences (conclusions drawn from our experiences about our descriptions of those experiences); and (c) between descriptions about descriptions, inferences based upon other inferences, affect about affect (feelings about other feelings), and between one person's abstractions and another person's abstractions, etc.

Bateson combined the notion of "orders of abstraction" with that of hierarchy of classification to identify several different levels of learning and change in animals and humans—each level synthesizing and integrating processes on the level below it and consequently having a greater degree of impact on the individual.

Bateson's Application of Logical Types to Play and Fantasy

Bateson first formally introduced the concept of "logical types" in his article A Theory of Play and Fantasy (1954). In it, Bateson argued that "play" involved distinguishing between different logical types of behavior and messages. Bateson noted that when animals and humans engage in "play" they often display the same behaviors that are also associated with aggression, sexuality, and other more "serious" aspects of life (such as when animals "play fight," or children play "doctor"). Yet, somehow, animals and humans were able to recognize, for the most part, that the play behavior was a different type or class of behavior and "not the real thing." According to Bateson, distinguishing between classes of behavior also required different types of messages. Bateson referred to these messages as "meta messages"—messages about other messages—claiming that they too were of a different "logical type" than the content of a particular communication. He believed that these "higher level" messages (which were usually communicated nonverbally) were crucial for people, and animals, to be able to communicate and interact effectively.

Animals at play, for instance, may signal the message "This is play" by wagging their tails, jumping up and down, or doing some other thing to indicate that what they are about to do is not to be taken for real. Their bite is a playful bite, not a real bite. Studies of humans also reveal the use of special messages that let others know they are playing in much the same way animals do. They may actually verbally "meta-communicate" by announcing that "This is only a game," or they laugh, wink, nudge, or do something odd to show their intent.

Figure (p.107)

The same behavior (growling) may be a member of two different classifications (play or aggression). Other behaviors serve as meta messages to show to which category a particular behavioral expression belongs.

Bateson claimed that many problems and conflicts were a result of the confusion or misinterpretation of these messages. A good example is the

— p. 106 —

difficulties that people from different cultures experience in interpreting the non-verbal subtleties of each other's communications.

Bateson's Application of Logical Types to Schizophrenia

In fact, Bateson next applied the concept of logical types as an explanation for some of the symptoms of serious psychological problems and mental illness. In Epidemiology of Schizophrenia (1955), Bateson maintained that the inability to correctly recognize and interpret meta messages, and to distinguish between different classes, or logical types, of behavior, was at the root of many seemingly psychotic or "crazy" behaviors. Bateson cited the example of a young mental patient who went into the pharmacy of the hospital where he was staying. The nurse behind the counter asked, "Can I help you?" The patient was unable to distinguish whether the communication was a threat, a sexual advance, an admonishment for being in the wrong place, or a genuine inquiry, etc.

When one is unable to make such distinctions, Bateson contended, that individual will end up, more often than not, acting in a way that is inappropriate for the situation. He likened it to a telephone switching system that was unable to distinguish the "country code" from the "city code" and the local telephone number. As a result, the switching system would inappropriately assign numbers belonging to the country code as part of the phone number, or parts of the phone number as the city code, etc. The consequence of this would be that, again more often than not, the dialer would get the "wrong number." Even though all of the numbers (the content) are correct, the classification of the numbers (the form) is confused, creating problems. [It should be noted that this is a fundamentally different communication problem than simply having "noise" on the telephone line which obscures the numbers. The causes of logical typing confusions are quite different than the causes of noisy signals.]

Figure (p.106)

Bateson likened hierarchies of logical typing in human communication to different classifications of numbers in a telephone switching system.

In Toward a Theory of Schizophrenia (co-authored with Don Jackson, Jay Haley and John Weakland, 1956), Bateson applied the notion of different logical types as a key element of the "double bind," which I presented earlier. As I pointed out, Bateson claimed that interpersonal double binds frequently resulted from confusions and paradoxes created by conflicting messages of different logical types.

Bateson believed that the ability to sort out the different logical types of messages and classifications which were at the root of such double binds was essential for effective therapy, and mental health in general. Bateson's ideas about the applications of the theory of logical types to communication and psychotherapy were further explored by his colleagues Watzlawick, Bavelas and Jackson in Pragmatics of Human Communication (1967).

— p. 105 —

Bateson's Applications of Logical Types and Logical Levels to the Process of Learning

Bateson's next application of the theory of logical types was to the process of learning. In The Logical Categories of Learning and Communication (1964) he extended the notion of logical typing to explain different types and phenomena of learning as well as communication. He defined two fundamental types, or levels, of learning which must be considered in all processes of change: "Learning I" (stimulus-response type conditioning) and "Learning II", or deutero learning, (learning to recognize the larger context in which the stimulus is occurring so that its meaning may be correctly interpreted). The most basic example of Learning II phenomena is set learning, or when an animal becomes "test-wise"—that is, laboratory animals will get faster and faster at learning new tasks that fall into the same class of activity. This has to do with learning classes of behavior rather than single isolated behaviors.

An animal trained in avoidance conditioning, for instance, will be able to learn different types of avoidance behavior more and more rapidly. It will, however, be slower at learning some "respondent" conditioned behavior (e.g., salivating at the sound of a bell) than some animal that has been conditioned in that class of behavior earlier. That is, it will learn quickly how to identify and stay away from objects that might have an electric shock associated with them but will be slower in learning to salivate when a bell rings. On the other hand, an animal trained in Pavlovian type conditioning will rapidly learn to salivate to new sounds and colors, etc., but will be slower to learn to avoid electrified objects.

Bateson pointed out that this ability to learn patterns or rules of a class of conditioning procedures was a different "logical type" of learning and did not function according to the same simple stimulus-response-reinforcement sequences used to learn specific isolated behaviors. Bateson noted, for instance, that the reinforcement for "exploration" (a means of learning-to-learn) in rats is of a different nature than that for the "testing" of a particular object (the learning content of exploration). He reports (Steps to an Ecology of Mind p. 282):

[Y]ou can reinforce a rat (positively or negatively) when he investigates a particular strange object, and he will appropriately learn to approach it or avoid it. But the very purpose of exploration is to get information about which objects should be approached or avoided. The discovery that a given object is dangerous is therefore a success in the business of getting information. The success will not discourage the rat from future exploration of other strange objects.

The whole purpose of exploring strange objects is to discover whether or not they are dangerous. So, getting punished for approaching a particular strange object does not stop the rat from exploring other strange objects to discover if they are safe to approach.

— p. 104 —

Figure (p.104)

According to Bateson, exploring an object to discover whether or not is safe is a different level of learning than avoiding objects that have been discovered to be dangerous or approaching those that have been discovered to be safe.

"Exploring" an object to discover if it is dangerous or safe and to be avoided or approached, then, is a different order of abstraction and at a different hierarchy of classification of learning than avoiding an object that has been discovered to be unsafe. The ability to explore, learn a discrimination task, or be creative is a higher level of learning than the specific behaviors that make up these abilities—and the dynamics and rules of change are different on this higher level.

As another example, our ability to generalize what we have learned functions in a different way than primary learning. Consider the experience of learning to write. Most readers probably learned to write using one their hands to laboriously master the specific finger, hand and arm movements necessary to form the individual letters on a piece of paper. Once mastered, however, this basic pattern can be transferred much more quickly to other parts of the body and different environmental contexts. For instance, each of us could probably easily make a recognizable letter "A" by tracing it in sand with our big toe. We can probably even use one of our elbows to outline the same letter on a wall or take a pencil in our teeth and create a recognizable facsimile of the letter on a piece of canvas.

What is impressive is that the particular groups and relationships of bones and muscles we use to move our feet, toes, elbows and neck are quite different than those in our hands and fingers, yet we can transfer what we have learned using one part of the body to many others. This is clearly a different level of learning than that of stimulus-response conditioning.

Bateson's Levels of Learning

Applying the theory of logical types and the notion of different orders of abstraction, Bateson identified a number of levels of learning—each responsible for making corrective changes and refinements in the lower level of learning upon which it operated.

Zero learning is characterized by specificity of response [i.e., having a specific behavior in a specific environment—RD] which—right or wrong—is not subject to correction.

Learning I is change in specificity of response by correction of errors of choice within a set of alternatives.

— p. 103 —

Learning II is a change in the process of Learning I, e.g., a corrective change in the set of alternatives from which choice is made, or it is a change in how the sequence is punctuated.

Learning III is change in the process of Learning II, e.g., a corrective change in the system of sets of alternatives from which choice is made.

(Steps to an Ecology of Mind p. 293)

Figure (p.103)

Different Levels of Learning Relate to Changes in Different Levels of Behavior

Using Bateson's earlier analogy of a telephone system, Learning 0 would be like an autodial that always dials the same number whether it successfully reaches the right person or not. Learning I would be a corrective change in the local number (altering the expression of specific behaviors). Learning II would be a corrective change in the area code (shifting the classification of objects or behaviors—from "safe" and "playful" to "dangerous" and "protective," for example). Learning III would be a corrective change in the country code (the larger system of classification).

Beyond these three levels, Bateson also hinted at the possibility of a Learning IV—a level of learning that he believed was not possible to achieve by any individual member of a species, but only collectively as a group or species as a whole. Learning IV would involve the establishment of completely new behaviors that do not fit any current system of classes of behavior. Learning IV would be a truly revolutionary type of learning that would involve the creation of whole new archetypes or systems of behavior.

When our ancestors stood up on two feet and spoke the first words, they were not selecting from some existing set of alternatives, nor were they modeling some other species or creature that already existed. They began something completely new that revolutionized our role on the planet.

Learning 0—No adjustment Learning I—Appropriately adjust local number Learning II—Appropriately adjust area code Learning III—Appropriately adjust country code

— p. 102 —

*Learning IV—Creating a new system of telephone numbers

An analogy for Bateson's levels of learning would be making adjustments to different parts of a telephone number in order to successfully reach the desired person.

Examples of Bateson's Levels of Learning

As an example of how these different levels might work, consider the situation of Pavlov and his dogs. Pavlov discovered that he could condition his dogs to salivate at the sound of a bell by repeatedly ringing the bell when he fed them. The dogs learned to associate the sound of the bell with receiving food. Pretty soon, all Pavlov needed to do was ring the bell and, even if he had no food, the dogs would begin to salivate, triggered by the sound alone.

According to Bateson's levels of learning model, the dogs' initial act of salivating when given food is a case of Learning Zero. It is a preprogrammed, instinctual response that is inherited and which would be difficult, if not impossible, to extinguish.

Learning to extend the reaction of salivation from the sight and smell of the food to the sound of the bell is an example of Learning I. Through repetition and reinforcement, the dog is learning to associate the specific response of salivating (as opposed to other responses, such as yawning, licking, blinking, etc.) with the specific stimulus of a particular bell.

Learning II would involve a "change in the set of alternatives from which choice is made."
