Chapter 3

Moshe Feldenkrais

Intelligence in Motion

Figure (p.176)

Moshe Feldenkrais

— p. 175 —

Chapter Overview

In this chapter, we will explore the work of Moshe Feldenkrais—an extraordinary innovator who expanded our understanding of human potential by revealing the deep intelligence inherent in the body. While many approaches to change focus primarily on the mind, Feldenkrais demonstrated that thinking, feeling, perception and movement are inseparably interconnected aspects of a single system.

His genius lay not simply in the techniques he developed—Functional Integration and Awareness Through Movement—but in the way he approached human functioning itself. Rather than imposing change, he elicited it by increasing awareness and expanding choice.

Mind and Body as One System

One of the foundational patterns in this chapter is the recognition that mind and body are not separate domains, but expressions of a unified cybernetic system. Feldenkrais showed that every thought has a physiological correlate and every movement reflects patterns of thinking and feeling. A change in one part of the system inevitably influences the whole.

This principle later became one of the foundations for what I developed as Somatic Syntax—the study of how movement patterns both reflect and shape cognitive processes.

Awareness as the Gateway to Change

Feldenkrais emphasized that awareness precedes transformation. Rather than correcting behavior directly, he focused on helping people become aware of how they were organizing their experience. As awareness increases, unconscious patterns become visible, new degrees of freedom emerge, and change occurs naturally through the discovery of new options. Without awareness, we simply repeat habitual patterns automatically.

The "How" Over the "What"

A hallmark of Feldenkrais' strategy is his focus on process rather than content. Instead of concentrating on what a person is doing, he explored how they were doing it. By changing the organization and structure of a pattern, the outcome changes organically. This subtle distinction becomes one of the most powerful themes in the chapter.

Exploring Limits to Discover Possibilities

Feldenkrais had a unique approach to working with physical limitations. He approached them not as obstacles to overcome through force, but as opportunities for learning. His strategy involved:

1. Exploring a function until a limitation was encountered 2. Increasing awareness of the pattern surrounding that limitation 3. Introducing small variations that opened new possibilities

— p. 174 —

Through this process, limitations become doorways to increased flexibility, awareness and choice.

Somatic Representation and "Thinking with the Body"

Another important theme is the understanding that the body itself functions as a representational system. Movement expresses relationships, beliefs, emotions and identity both literally and metaphorically. This extends the traditional NLP notion of representational systems into a more embodied domain—what might be called thinking with the whole system.

Somatic Syntax: The Structure of Movement

Building on Feldenkrais' work, this chapter also introduces the principles of Somatic Syntax. Just as verbal language has syntax, so does body language. Exploring somatic syntax shifts focus from which body parts are involved to the patterns and relationships between posture, gesture, breathing and movement. By altering the syntax of these relationships, we change their meaning—and ultimately the deeper experience they represent.

Metacognition Through Movement

Feldenkrais' methods provide a powerful pathway to metacognition, or awareness of one's own thinking processes. Through subtle shifts in movement and attention, people begin to recognize deeper cognitive and emotional patterns while developing greater self-regulation and adaptability.

Feldenkrais' "Working Picture" of a Human Being

One of the most elegant aspects of Feldenkrais' strategy was his use of what he called his working picture of a human being—a dynamic internal model integrating the nervous system, the body and the environment. This systemic perspective allowed him to identify disruptions in functioning and introduce changes that restored flow and organization. In many ways, this mirrors the principles of Third Generation NLP and Generative Change.

The Iterative Feedback Loop of Change

At the heart of Feldenkrais' genius was an ongoing cycle between internal imagery, conceptual understanding and sensory feedback. This continuous refinement process resembles what NLP would recognize as modeling in action. Rather than forcing change, the system evolves organically through awareness, experimentation and feedback.

The Deeper Invitation

Ultimately, this chapter invites readers to explore a form of intelligence—somatic intelligence—that operates differently from purely cognitive understanding. Feldenkrais teaches us to learn directly from experience, increase flexibility in both mind and body, and trust the self-organizing intelligence of the human system.

For me personally, Feldenkrais was pivotal in expanding NLP beyond the "neuro-linguistic" into the neuro-somatic. His work helped me realize that sustainable generative change must include the body as an essential participant in the process.

Feldenkrais reminds us that transformation does not come from forcing ourselves to become different, but from becoming more aware of how we are

— p. 173 —

already being. By increasing awareness, introducing variation and restoring choice, we uncover possibilities that were always present but previously unnoticed.

Figure (p.173)

Moshe Feldenkrais viewed the human body as a system that can be pictured, and understood to unblock the flows of energy.

— p. 172 —

Moshe Feldenkrais

Moshe Feldenkrais (1904 – 1984) has been recognized as one of the most important innovators in the fields of bodywork, physical therapy and somatic healing. Trained as a physicist, Feldenkrais worked as a research assistant for Nobel laureates Frédéric and Irène Joliot-Curie in Paris before World War II and helped to smuggle the results of their atomic research out of France during the German occupation, delivering it personally to Winston Churchill. He developed his own form of martial art to help the settlers involved in the establishment of the state of Israel and is the only Westerner to have a move incorporated into the ancient martial art of Judo.

Feldenkrais' fame, and the primary arena for his genius, came from his work with people with difficult physical handicaps, such as cerebral palsy, stroke, spinal injury, scoliosis, etc. For example, I have a video of a session of him working with boy with cerebral palsy and in twenty minutes correct a muscular problem that three different physicians claimed would require major surgery to address.

Feldenkrais' remarkable success rate in the rehabilitation of people with serious physical problems and "miracle" cures with those unreachable by standard Western medical treatments brought him an international reputation.

Feldenkrais codified his discoveries about working with the body into a group of techniques called Functional Integration and Awareness Through Movement. While these techniques are very effective, our interest in exploring Feldenkrais' strategy of genius is more in the source of the techniques than the techniques themselves. It was Feldenkrais' ability to innovate within the basic parameters of his work that allowed him to evolve his methods and determine the unique but fundamental needs of each of his individual patients.

The Connection of Moshe Feldenkrais to NLP

Like Erickson and Bateson, Moshe Feldenkrais' was another significant influence on the early development of NLP. He was also an important force in the evolution of what I call the "second generation" of NLP, which involved the inclusion of the body as well as the brain in the development of NLP methods and techniques. Like NLP, Feldenkrais asserted the profound truth that "thought, feeling, perception and movement are closely interrelated and influence each other."

NLP co-founders Bandler and Grinder were fascinated by Feldenkrais' work and published two of his books – The Elusive Obvious (1981) and The Master Moves (1984) – through their publishing company Meta Publications. Their appreciation of him and his work is evident in Richard Bandler's dedication to Feldenkrais in The Master Moves:

It was an honor to know you.
A joy to share your friendship,
and a privilege to publish your books.
— p. 171 —

Depending upon one's orientation, Feldenkrais' methods have been called NLP of the body and NLP has been called Feldenkrais of the mind. There are many parallels between the principles and processes applied by the two methodologies. For example, each of them developed "heuristically" – meaning as a result of direct observation, practice and application, rather than theoretical constructs. Both NLP and Feldenkrais methods emphasize the importance of observation and self-awareness. As Feldenkrais wrote in The Elusive Obvious:

I believe that we are able to live the way we want. The main obstacle is ignorance: scientific ignorance, personal ignorance, and cultural ignorance. If we do not know what we are actually enacting then we cannot possibly do what we want. . . I believe that knowing oneself is the most important thing a human being can do for himself. . . By learning to act not as one should, but as one does.

In particular, NLP and Feldenkrais practice bring attention to how we are thinking and acting. In Feldenkrais' words:

The "how" is the hallmark of our individuality; it is an inquiry into the process of acting. If we look at how we do things, we might find alternative ways of doing them, i.e., have some free choice. For, if we have no alternative, we have no choice at all.

The emphasis on self-awareness as a pathway to choice and change has been a hallmark of NLP since its foundation. A key principle of NLP is that those aspects of our inner programming we are not aware of essentially run us. When we are aware of our beliefs and strategies we can update or replace them by others that may be more effective. In fact, that is a major part of what this Strategies of Genius series is all about. By becoming aware of and modeling effective strategies developed by exceptional people we have other alternatives to the strategies that we have developed largely unconsciously.

Shared Presuppositions of NLP and Feldenkrais

As we shall see, similar to Erickson and Bateson, Feldenkrais' work shares many of the presuppositions of NLP including:

1. Mind and body are part of the same cybernetic structure and anything that occurs in one part of the system will affect the other parts.

2. The map is not the territory. Everyone perceives and acts from their own unique model of the world.

3. People have within themselves all the resources and potential they need to take the next steps in solving their problems and achieving their goals.

4. The facilitator's task is to find and draw out resources, rather than simply give advice or suggestions.

5. There is a distinction between the person and the behavior and the behavior and the intention behind it.

6. At the highest level, all intentions are positive and, therefore, even behavior that appears negative has somewhere a positive and useful purpose in the system.

— p. 170 —

7. Communication between people and communication between the psychological and physical aspects within each person is a cybernetic system (rather than a mechanical, linear cause-effect system).

8. Memory and imagination use similar neurological circuits and can have similar impact.

9. People make the best choices available to them given their unique (but often limited) model of the world and their perceptions of the particular situation.

10. The purpose of effective change is to add more choices, not take any away.

My Personal Experience with Feldenkrais and His Methodology

I met and interacted with Moshe Feldenkrais a number of times during the period he was being studied by Bandler and Grinder. As with Milton Erickson, I also watched numerous videos of Feldenkrais in action and read his books. I was impressed with the results he was able to produce with his work. As a student of both physics and human potential myself, I always felt a lot of resonance with Feldenkrais' work. He was a major influence on my integrating more and more somatic intelligence into my developments in NLP.

Beyond my personal contact with Feldenkrais, I had extensive ongoing experience with two of his most long-term dedicated and creative students: Kolman Korentayer and Mia Segal.

Kolman Korentayer was a native of Montreal, Canada. In the early 1960s he ran a coffee shop in the city where he hosted folk musicians, including then unknown folk legends like Bob Dylan and Leonard Cohen. By chance, he got a contract to provide catering for the famous Expo 67 in Montreal and earned enough money to become financially independent. He used the opportunity to explore the burgeoning world of self-development in the late 1960s, traveling to the Esalen Institute in California and becoming a student of Fritz Perls. It was through his passion for personal development that he became acquainted with Feldenkrais and his work. In fact, Kolman was key to bringing Feldenkrais to North America.

Kolman moved to Santa Cruz in the early 1980s and we became friends. We spent many hours discussing Feldenkrais, his work, his way of looking at behavioral change and how that could be applied to various issues other than physical problems. This led us to develop several products to offer support to people wanting to be creative or make some type of change. These products all involved physically manipulatable objects whose purpose was to involve some type of movement along with a cognitive strategy. These included:

Choice Cigarettes – based on my Smoking Cessation Strategy (see Encyclopedia of Systemic NLP, pp. 1249-1254). It consisted of a pack of fake cigarettes that could be held like an actual cigarette in order to satisfy the need to do something with one's hands that smokers often report as being part of the appeal of smoking. However, each faux cigarette had printed on the side another possible choice to make instead of smoking. These included instructions such as:

- Close your eyes, take a deep breath and relax.

— p. 169 —

- Do something physically healthy and enjoyable like stretching, taking a quick walk or jog, etc. - Take 3 minutes and fantasize about something you would really enjoy. - Create something. Draw a picture, fold some paper. - Go talk to someone near that you are really interested in. - Etc.

Writer's Cubes – based on my Composition Strategy (see Encyclopedia of Systemic NLP, pp. 203-207). The Writing Cubes Program was a multi-sensory, hands-on program which stimulated creative and productive writing. The core of the program involved key words placed on the faces of specially designed cubes. One cube had a group of verbal "connectives" (i.e., because, before, after, whenever, even though). A next cube had a series of perspectives (I, you, we, they, he, she). A third cube contained particular sensory representational systems (see, hear, feel, show, say, move).

Figure (p.169)

Writer's Cubes

The purpose of the cubes was to help people focus their attention and then stimulate and link thinking processes through movement and sensory-motor activity. The user applied the words and cubes to help generate sentences and ideas while writing. The key words on the cubes could be organized in specific sequences – e.g., because I see... or after they say.... whenever you feel... etc. – in order to draw out particular pathways of associations or could be referred to randomly in order to increase spontaneity.

By connecting touch and movement with the other senses during learning, the Writing Cubes Program added significantly to the educational experience. The Writing Cubes Program allowed students to learn kinesthetically, visually and auditorily and helped them to enrich their variety of expression.

Belief Blox – based on my Belief Audit (see Encyclopedia of Systemic NLP, pp. 106-109). Applying a similar structure to the Writing Cubes Program, the purpose of Belief Blox was to help people to strengthen key beliefs by using a sequence of key words on the cubes organized in specific sequences in order to draw out particular pathways of associations.

Belief Blox

— p. 168 —

Belief Blox

I had an even closer partnership with Mia Segal, one of Feldenkrais' earliest and most experienced and exceptional students – who became his assistant in 1957 and worked with him intimately for decades. Mia and I traded sessions in which she would work with me and my family members using Feldenkrais methods and I would then coach her and her family members applying NLP. We then began co-working with clients, trading off applying the two methodologies. This led to us developing a seminar series that combined NLP and Feldenkrais' methods. For many years, in the late 1980's and early 1990's, Mia and I co-taught programs in Switzerland, The Netherlands, England, France, Germany and the United States integrating NLP and the Feldenkrais approach.

This, of course, gave me an in-depth experiential understanding of the principles and practice of Feldenkrais' work that has stayed with me as an inspiration for my own work ever since then and has been the source of a number of my developments over the years.

One of the exercises I developed as a result of my work with Mia – and inspired by Feldenkrais' notions of 'awareness through movement' – was the following process to develop greater metacognition (see Encyclopedia of Systemic NLP, pp. 719-720).

Developing Metacognition

Feldenkrais emphasized the importance of self-awareness for being able "to live the life we want." Another term for self-awareness is metacognition.

Metacognition is the ability to be introspectively aware of one's own thoughts and behavior. It is "cognition about cognition." Metacognition should be distinguished from 'self-consciousness'. Unlike self-consciousness, metacognition does not come from a seemingly separate 'self' who judges and interferes with the process under observation. Metacognition involves only the awareness of the steps of one's thought process and its connection to one's behavior. In fact, metacognition will often only reach consciousness after the thought process is completed.

Metacognition is also different from taking a typical "meta" position, in which you become a disassociated observer of yourself from a distance. Developing metacognition involves staying associated in your own experience but being aware of its structure at the same time.

The value of metacognition is that, by becoming aware of how you are thinking and how that connects to your actions (micro and macro), you are able to constantly validate or correct your inner thinking strategies. To use a computer analogy, most of the time a computer user does not see nor care about the programs that are making the computer function. But if you want to improve the functioning of the computer, "fix a bug" in its functioning or translate the program for a different type of computer, you need to be able to view and trace the set of instructions that make up the program.

Metacognition is an essential ability in both NLP and Feldenkrais work. It has applications for therapy, learning, modelling and improving communication. Metacognition helps us to become more aware of our inner cognitive strategies, mental maps, inferences and assumptions and how they influence and are influenced by our behavior. When applied to

— p. 167 —

therapy, communication and conflict resolution, metacognition helps people to become aware of the aspects of their strategies, mental maps, inferences and assumptions that may be different from others, or in error, and thus at the source of the conflict or problem. Many NLP techniques require people to become more aware of the qualities of their inner imagery, internal voice, or feelings. Feldenkrais' Awareness Through Movement practices do the same thing with respect to our bodies and our physical movements and sensations. By increasing these aspects of self-awareness, people are able to develop better inner feedback and more choices with respect to their thoughts and actions.

Figure (p.167)

Exercise: Strategy for Developing 'Metacognition'

1. Identify two states that are important for you to recognize and respond to in relation to some desired outcome, but which are different.

e.g., 'open to learn' and 'closed to learning' or 'COACH state' versus 'CRASH state'

2. Create two spatial locations representing the two states. Arrange the two locations to be at some distance from one another.

3. Lay out 10 steps that represent a sequence of intermediate increments of change that would occur as you move from one state to the other.

4. Fully associate yourself into one of the states. Take a step toward the other state, imagining how your state would begin to change as you shifted in the direction of the other state.

a. examine the changes in physiology that occur as you take that step.

b. examine the changes in cognitive patterns that occur as you take that step.

5. Continue to take incremental steps toward the other state, examining the changes in physical and cognitive patterns, until you have fully reached the other state.

— p. 166 —

Figure (p.166)

6. Begin to walk back toward the first state along the incremental steps. Stop at a point that is exactly in between the two states. Notice that this is an important choice point. How will you be able to recognize it in the future?

7. Finish returning to the state from which you started. Go to a 'meta position' and reflect on what you have learned.

Feldenkrais' Strategy for Exploring Functions and Their Limitations

This metacognition exercise reflects one of Feldenkrais' key strategies for exploring particular physical functions and their boundaries; i.e., what kinds of things do human bodies do and what are their limits (or perceived limits). The following exercise is an example of an application of that strategy that can help you to create more awareness and flexibility through movement.

1. Explore a physical function until you find a limitation. (This could be something as simple as turning your head until you feel it is impossible to turn it any further, or to bend down to touch your toes until you feel you cannot go any lower.

2. Increase your awareness (widen perceptual space to include more of your body) about that function/limitation. (I.e., Notice which parts of your body you are involving in making the movement and what your assumptions are about what it takes to make that movement.)

3. Find alternatives that move you in new possibilities. (e.g. Noticing how your shoulder blades play a role in turning your head or how your cervical vertebrae and neck muscles are involved in being able to touch your toes.)

These same steps can equally be applied to cognitive functioning, and to the interaction between mind and body. It was this strategy that led me to the development of what is known as Somatic Syntax in NLP.

— p. 165 —

Figure (p.165)

Somatic Syntax

Somatic Syntax is a set of principles and interventions that I developed with Judith DeLozier (who, as a trained dancer was also intrigued by Feldenkrais' work) – a co-author with Bandler, Grinder and myself of NLP Volume 1, co-author with John Grinder of Turtles All the Way Down, and with me of The Encyclopedia of Systemic NLP and NLP II: The Next Generation.

Somatic Syntax has to do with the organization of our physiology and "body language," and its influence on our cognitive functioning. In the spirit of Feldenkrais' work, somatic syntax focuses more on the form and deeper organization of body dynamics than it does on its content. The term "somatic" comes from the Greek word soma, which means "body." Syntax is a Greek word meaning "to put in order" or "arrange."

Thus, Somatic Syntax is not concerned so much with which parts of the body are involved as it is with the underlying pattern, form and relationships between the way we hold and move any particular parts of our body. Like Feldenkrais' approach, rather than focus on physical details Somatic Syntax emphasizes the overall pattern and organization of movement and the formation of cognitive-somatic (i.e., "mind-body") expressions.

The Body as a Representational System

The perspective of earlier generations of NLP had been that all our information about the world around us is relayed by the senses to the brain, where it is centrally represented and processed. One of the tenets of Somatic Syntax is that the body itself is a "representational system." Rather than considering the body as simply some kind of mechanical shell for inputting and outputting signals to and from the brain, Somatic Syntax views the body as a means for representing and processing information; within the "belly brain", "heart brain" and other nervous system structures within the body.

According to Somatic Syntax, we can use our bodies to make a model of the world just as we do with our other representational systems (visual, auditory, kinesthetic). We can represent key relationships in the world around us and in our personal history in the relationship between parts of our body. Feldenkrais, for example, believed that our perception of the relationship between our mother and father, and our relationship with them, could be represented by the relationship between our left and right hands, or between our chest and our belly, or in the movement of our hips. This is a profoundly powerful observation.

— p. 164 —

In addition to being able to input, process and output information, all representational systems have the capability to represent information in at least two ways: literally and figuratively. That is, each of our sensory systems can form maps that have either a direct correspondence or a more metaphorical connection to the phenomenon we are representing. For example, we can visualize the white cells of our bodies as we have seen them under the microscope, or as looking like octopi or video game characters. Similarly, we can speak of our brains literally as "a network of neurons," or figuratively as being "like a computer." Likewise, we can experience a particular emotional symptom as a certain set of kinesthetic body sensations or as a "knot" in the stomach.

As a representational system, our bodies share this double capacity. We can express movements that are the literal response to a particular situation, or create expressions that are more metaphorical, as in dance and mime. A state of anxiety, for instance, may be literally represented by reproducing the physical effects which accompany a feeling of anxiety (such as tensing up the muscles in one's face and shoulders), or figuratively represented by placing one's arms over one's head and eyes, as if hiding from something dangerous.

As is the case with our other representational modalities, metaphorical representations are often more meaningful and impactful (because they carry multiple levels of information). According to Gregory Bateson, the metaphoric mode of representation characterized by Somatic Syntax is the primary way in which most animals communicate. An adult male wolf, for example, may treat another adult male wolf with the same behavior used by a mother wolf toward her cub, as a sign of rapprochement or dominance.

Applying Somatic Syntax

Somatic Syntax is the active study of how movement may be used to help bring knowledge into the muscle, and to help draw out knowledge from the muscle. It is a means to access the "wisdom of the body." According to Somatic Syntax, repetitive structures of movement can form the framework surrounding a particular thinking process and thus influence its consequences. As Moshe Feldenkrais pointed out (in Body and Mature Behavior, 1949):

A recurrent emotional state always appears together with the attitude of the body and the vegetative state with which it was conditioned earlier. Therefore, when an individual emotional complex has been resolved, a specifically individual body habit is resolved simultaneously.

Feldenkrais deeply understood that the manifestation of our thoughts and feelings must eventually be reflected in our body or physiology in some way. Our mental and emotional responses become manifested into the world through patterns in our words, voice tone, facial expression, body posture, the movement of our hands, arms, legs, etc.

Similarly, shifting these physical patterns can also influence our mental and emotional states. That is the essence of Somatic Syntax.

Somatic Syntax Exercises

— p. 163 —

The following exercises explore ways to apply the principles and practice pioneered by Moshe Feldenkrais to deepen and enrich internal resources, model resources from others, communicate more effectively and transform problematic responses. (See Encyclopedia of Systemic NLP, pp. 423-425)

Exercise 1: Getting a Resource "In the Muscle"

This exercise helps to identify particular patterns of movements associated with a resourceful internal state; in particular those that operate to enhance or diminish that state.

1. Identify a resource state (creative, confident, centered, etc.) that you would like to have more of in your life.

2. Fully experience the resource and identify any spontaneous physical expressions associated with a resource state (i.e., body posture, breathing pattern, movements, gestures, etc.).

3. Explore the organization (deep structure) of the physical expressions by changing the "syntax" or form of different aspects of them (i.e., quality, speed, parts of the body involved, sequence, direction, etc.).

4. Notice which changes:

a. Intensify/make more of the state.

b. Dampen/make less of the state.

c. Change the state to a different state.

Exercise 2: Generalizing the Resourceful Pattern

This exercise helps to increase access to a particular internal state by incorporating key patterns of movements associated with that state into other actions and activities.

Choose three common actions or "macro" behaviors (e.g., walking, carrying something, sitting, writing). For each action:

1. Start making your best version of the physical expressions associated with the resource state you have explored in the previous exercise.

2. Begin the action (walking, sitting, etc.) and adapt the physical expression of the resource (breath, posture, gestures, movements, etc.) to blend with and fit that activity in the way that is most natural and preserves the full experience of the resource.

Exercise 3: Applying the Resourceful Pattern

In this exercise, you take the pattern associated with the resource you have explored in the first two exercises into situations in which it would be (or would have been) useful.

1. Identify a specific context or situation in which you would like to have more access to the resource you have been exploring in the previous exercises.

2. Internally project yourself into the situation, remembering or imagining what is like to actually be in that context. Notice the body posture, breathing pattern and movements that spontaneously emerge as you put yourself into the situation. Physically express the posture, breath, movement, etc.

— p. 162 —

3. Blend the physical expression of the resource that you have been exploring with the pattern of movement spontaneously associated with that situation. Notice what begins to shift in your posture, breath and quality of movement.

4. Adapt the physical expression of the resource so that it integrates and "fits" into the situation most appropriately and elegantly.

5. Notice how bringing the resource movement pattern into the situation transforms or enriches your experience of that situation.

Figure (p.162)

The Fractal Dragon

(Benoit B. Mandelbrot/IBM)

"Somatic Fractal" Format

A fractal is a complex geometric pattern that can be subdivided into parts, each of which is (at least approximately) a smaller copy of the whole. Fractals are generally "self-similar" (the bits look like the whole) and independent of scale (they look similar, no matter how close you zoom in). Benoit Mandelbrot, the discoverer of the Mandelbrot set, coined the term "fractal" in 1975 from the Latin fractus meaning "to break." Because fractals are generally made of irregular curves or shapes repeated at every scale they are difficult to represent with classical geometry and have developed into their own branch of mathematics.

Many mathematical structures are fractals; e.g. the Sierpinski triangle, Koch snowflake, Peano curve, Mandelbrot set and Lorenz attractor. Fractals also describe many real-world objects that do not have simple geometric shapes, such as clouds, mountains, turbulence, and coastlines. Fractal type equations are also used as the generative engines behind many "artificial life" simulations.

— p. 161 —

The Sierpinski Triangle

From an NLP perspective, fractals are a good example of how a simple process at the level of deep structure can generate a complex pattern at the level of surface structure. Many behaviors could be considered to be types of "neurolinguistic" fractals. Dance, for instance is a type of somatic fractal. Many forms of music are a type of auditory fractal (consider Ravel's Bolero, Pacelbel's Canon in D or Bach's fugues for example).

Feldenkrais intuitively understood that many physical functions had fractal-like qualities. That is, the same quality of movement (opening and closing, expanding and contracting, spiraling, etc.) could be expressed at a macro level or micro level with different parts of the body. For example, you can make a circle with your little finger, your whole hand, your arm, your torso, and so on.

The following exercise combines Somatic Syntax the principles of the fractal and Feldenkrais awareness through mov to creating and enhancing personal resource states.

Creating a Somatic Fractal for a Resource State

1. Identify and associate into a resourceful state (e.g., creativity, confidence, focus, etc.)

2. As you fully "relive" what it is like to be in this state, notice a physical pattern or quality of movement that accompanies the resource state. What sensations are there? Where are they in your body? What is the pattern and quality of movement?

3. Mindfully make a few subtle variations in this movement and notice the impact they have on your experience of the resourceful feeling. This will help you get the sense of its deeper structure.

4. Transfer the pattern and quality of movement to some other part of your body. If the movement naturally involved your arms, for instance, transfer it to your shoulders. Make any adaptations you need to until it seems natural, and you can feel the sense of the resourceful state as a result of making the movement with this other part of your body.

5. Transfer the resourceful movement to as many parts of your body as you can (i.e., face, feet, eyes, breathing, hips, etc.). In this way, your whole body should be alive with the resource.

Feldenkrais' Strategy of Genius for Resolving Physiological Problems

The modeling tools of NLP also provide a way to gain direct insight into the mental processes that guided Feldenkrais' gifted hands. By exploring the way in which he organized his internal experience while working with clients we can create an explicit map of how Feldenkrais determined what to do to help them.

One clue to the way Feldenkrais approached his work is contained in his statement that:

In every event there are three features to be considered: 1) the perception, which is the realization of the material fact of a sensory nerve irradiation; 2) the sensation, which is the integration of
— p. 160 —
the state of self at the moment that the irradiation has reached
and affected the nervous system; and finally, 3) the psycho-somatic
(mind-body) reaction.

This view is completely aligned with NeuroLinguistic Programming. In fact, we can say that Feldenkrais is describing the "neuro" aspect of a "program."

The Basic Steps of Feldenkrais' Strategy

One of the most explicit descriptions of Feldenkrais' organizational strategy comes from his book The Case of Nora, in which he describes in detail his work with a woman who had been severely incapacitated by a stroke – left unable to read, write or even tie her shoes. Yet, with Feldenkrais' help, she was able to resume normal functioning after just a few months, much to the amazement of her family, friends and doctors. At one point in the narrative Feldenkrais describes how he was able to figure out a way around a particularly difficult impasse in Nora's progress. In this discussion he states:

When I am presented with a trouble in function, I make a special effort not to think in words. I try not to think logically and in correctly formed sentences. It has become a habit with me to imagine the relevant nervous structures by seeing them with my mind's eye. I imagine a part which produces a flow of fluid. Part of the travel of the fluid is electrical, then becomes chemical, and again electrical. After many transformations the flow will end in a muscular contraction, and the muscular play will result in some apparent outside action involving the body, or parts of it, that will affect or transform the immediate environment.

This description provides a powerful insight into Feldenkrais micro strategy for addressing physiological problems. Like a number of other geniuses that I've studied, Feldenkrais orients toward constructed visual imagery (Vᶜ) as the central process for gathering information in his strategy. Albert Einstein, for example, claimed that his primary thinking process took place through visual imagery and stated, "I very rarely think in words at all."

Feldenkrais called this mental image his "working picture of a human being." This picture contained three primary elements: (1) the nervous system (the "core"); (2) the skeleton, viscera and muscles (the "envelope") and (3) physical space, the social milieu and gravitation (the "environment"). Feldenkrais claimed that no conception of a human being could be complete which did not include these elements and their dynamic interdependence. In his description above we are able to determine exactly how he represented that interdependence to himself in a very elegant manner – the nervous system effects the body which effects the external environment which feeds back into the nervous system.

Notice, by the way, the importance of the particular sequence and hierarchy of elements Feldenkrais uses in his "working picture." Had the sequence gone [external environment]g[nervous system]g[body], he would have been functioning as a behaviorist (that is the sequence Pavlov studied with his dogs and Skinner with his pigeons). If the sequence had been [body]g[external environment]g[nervous system] his system may have been more similar to a chiropractor or a medical doctor. Just having this picture, however, does not yet allow us to be able to find and solve the

— p. 159 —

root of a difficult physical problem. In his description of working with Nora, Feldenkrais goes on to say:

Sometimes I am stuck at a point where I cannot imagine the pattern of the flow, nor the possible obstacles in its way. Then I ask, is the obstacle a diffusion, damping, deviation, loss of impetus, break of continuity, or an impossibility of one of the transformations?

It is at this point that words (Aᵈ) come into play in the form of a question with a list of six possible verbal categories to describe the gap or stuck point. Here Feldenkrais' systems-oriented approach is quite evident. The six possibilities he mentions cover the basic categories of what could happen to the transfer of energy or information in any interconnected system. For example, with some modifications, this very same strategy could be used to help business consultants discover what problems might be occurring in office communication systems, order filling procedures or in a particular action plan.

Diffusion occurs when information has been scattered over too many different elements and may no longer be strong or clear enough to be useful. Damping occurs when there is some resistance to the passage of information along the way and its flow has become reduced to a trickle. A deviation occurs when information is turned in the wrong direction and is unable to reach its appropriate connection point. Loss of impetus, as opposed to "damping," occurs when the information or energy has been reduced at its source as opposed to encountering resistance along the way. A break of continuity would occur when a particular connection has become separated and can no longer pass on the information. Finally, an impossibility of one of the transformations occurs when, even though the connection is still there, one of the mechanisms needed to translate or transform the information into its appropriate form to be passed between two elements has been damaged or lost.

As Feldenkrais goes on to say:

I have found this way of imagining so fruitful that I cannot do without it. It often shows me where my knowledge is insufficient so that I know exactly what I am after and therefore in which books I am likely to find the information. I form a working theory and change it in the light of new observations. This gives me a fair idea of what data I lack and what observations I must add to make the theory work.

This description indicates the interplay between visual (Vᶜ) and verbal (Aᵈ) representations in Feldenkrais' strategy. It seems that the visual image "shows" him what is missing and the verbal labels help him to classify it and to gather information from outside sources. Most likely, he uses this new information to complete the picture (Vᶜ) – his new "working theory." The feedback loop is completed as he makes new external observations that confirm or challenge the validity of his internal theory. These observations are most likely initiated through some kind of tactile contact (Kᵉ) and visual observation (Vᵉ) as Feldenkrais touches and moves the body. The result he sees casts new "light" (Vᶜ) on his working picture.

One thing that is certainly remarkable about this feedback loop is that it is designed to locate what he does not know, something that would be

— p. 158 —

embarrassing or threatening to many. In fact, notice how it is the reverse of the typical strategy in which observations are evaluated on the basis of how they fit into the theory, as opposed to how the theory fits the observations. This ability to search for what one does not know instead of simply applying what one does know is a very important element of what made Feldenkrais such an innovator and so successful in unique and difficult cases. As he mentions himself, it also serves the important function of directing him to where to make his next external observations.

He goes on to point out:

This mode of thinking is often successful in situations where specialists with greater knowledge than mine have failed. Nobody is omniscient enough to think mechanically. I start each case as if it were my first and ask myself more questions than any of my assistants or critics ever do.

What Feldenkrais is saying here is that the "mode" or process of gathering, organizing, and evaluating information is more important than the specific "knowledge" that serves as the content of that process. By starting each case as if it were his first, Feldenkrais is more aware, more creative, has more contact with the patient and does not fall prey to limiting presuppositions that may in the end not be valid.

Summary of Feldenkrais' Problem-Solving Strategy

In summary, Feldenkrais' problem-solving strategy is composed of a feedback loop that has the following representational structure:

Figure (p.158)

- Information is accessed through an attempt to form a constructed visual image or "working picture" (Vᶜ) that follows the sequencing of three basic elements – the nervous system (the core), the body system (the envelope) and external context (the environment). Each part of the system is connected to the other and information is passed from one to the other through certain types of transformations.

The purpose of the image is to identify gaps and difficulties in the connections and transformations of information between the three interdependent elements of the working picture.

— p. 157 —

- These difficulties are then organized and classified verbally (Aᵈ) into one of six possible categories: 1) a diffusion, 2) a damping, 3) a deviation, 4) a loss of impetus, 5) a break of continuity or 6) an impossibility of one of the transformations. New information is gathered, typically in verbal form that leads to a theory for getting around the difficulty.

- This theory is represented as a change in the dynamics of the "working picture" (Vᶜ).

- The validity of the theory is then evaluated based on external observations made through the hands and eyes (Kᵉ→Vᵉ) as one tries to implement the changes suggested by the theory. These observations are then related back to the internal image as feedback and the whole process cycles again.

The beauty of Feldenkrais' strategy lies in its simplicity and its generalizability to almost any information processing system. It is neither simply a checklist nor the application of previous information as a diagnostic device but rather a dynamic interplay between theory and observation—a process that in NLP is called Modeling. It is an ongoing process that is designed to identify and fill gaps in one's understanding of the functioning of the whole system that may be easily adapted to individual cases.

I have adapted Feldenkrais' process to a general problem-solving strategy that may be applied in many different kinds of problem situations. Instead of visualizing chemical transformation of fluid, I borrowed a little bit from Einstein and created the visualization of the information in the system as light. The basic procedure may be outlined as follows:

Applying Moshe Feldenkrais' Problem Identification and Solution Strategy

1. Identify an experience where you have reached an impasse and select a physical location to be associated with it. Step into the physical location you have chosen and put yourself into that experience as fully as possible. Represent the impasse with your body as gestures and movements.

2. Step away from the physical location and visualize the whole system involved in the impasse experience. Include: (a) the information carrying system (core); (b) the mechanical elements of the system (envelope); (c) the physical and social context (environment).

3. Visualize the information influencing the system as colors flowing through the system. Find where the flow gets blocked or stuck.

4. Identify if it is a (a) diffusion, (b) damping, (c) deviation, (d) loss of impetus, (e) break in the carrier or (f) impossibility of transformation.

5. Find a reference experience for a functional system and select a different physical location for it. Step into the location you have chosen and put yourself into the positive reference experience. Represent the functional system with your body in gestures and movements.

6. Step away from the location and visualize the differences in the information flow in that system as colors flowing through the system. Be sure to visualize the interaction between the core, envelope and environment.

— p. 156 —

7. Bring your attention back to the impasse situation. Using your representation of the functional reference experience as guide, visualize a way to (a) focus the information, (b) clear the information path, (c) keep the information on track, (d) Intensify the information signal, (e) repair or reconnect the information carrier, or (f) get around, over, under, etc., the impossible transform area in the impasse experience.

Figure (p.156)

Problem Solution
DiffusionRefocus
DampingRemove Resistance
DeviationRedirect
Loss of ImpetusStrengthen
Break of ContinuityReconnect
Impossibility of TransformationFind a New Pathway

8. Using your imagination, transfer the light pattern from the resourceful reference image to the impasse image.

9. Associate back into the impasse image to find out how the changes you have made to the system have affected your experience of the problem and to explore how you might translate the light pattern into concrete actions.

An Example of Applying Feldenkrais' Problem Identification and Solution Strategy

As I have pointed out, part of the genius of Feldenkrais's strategy is that it can be adapted to many different types of systems. The following is a transcript from a seminar I gave on the Strategies of Genius that illustrates how Feldenkrais' process may be utilized with an individual seeking to solve a business problem:

R.D.: Is there somebody who has a problem, or a dream that they are working on? It doesn't matter what it is.

A volunteer comes up

R.D.: Is it possible to say in general what the space of the problem is?

Woman: It is a business problem.

R.D.: OK, good. Put yourself into the experience where you have reached the impasse. Find that point where you can't move any further. Then step into this location over here and associate into it and show with your body what happens.

— p. 155 —

(Woman stands in the location, her body is slumped forward, facial muscles tense and breathing is shallow.)

R.D.: OK, now step out. And we are going to try to think like Feldenkrais, which means not verbally, not logically. No sound, forget all the words in there. There are too many of them anyway.

Figure (p.155)

And I will ask you to visualize what all the elements of the system are. But visualize all the connections between the elements as a kind of painting of light.

For example, I had a person who said, "I have a lot of problems in my company. I have a team of people who are trying to develop products, and I tell them what they have to do and I present them the vision. They get excited and they follow me. But then, they don't follow through with it. If I am not there, they lose it."

So I said, "Alright, lets visualize this. How do you see it?"

He said, "Well, I have this red light, really bright red light. It is coming out of my head to their heads. And their heads get bright. But when I go away that red light gets dimmer and dimmer."

As another example, I was working with a woman who was a special education teacher having problems with a student. She just couldn't get through to that individual.

When I had her describe her picture of the impasse she said, "I have this kind of white light, it is coming up out of here at the top of my head, and I try to get it into that child's ears, down into him. But there is a black hole in there and the light just disappears, it can't reach in there."

— p. 154 —

Figure (p.154)

I asked, "Is that the total problem space?" And we were kind of looking at the space and all of a sudden she saw the child's family in a real darkness and that black hole was connected to the family. That was the visualization.

So lets start by how you might visualize your impasse. Is there more than one person?

Woman: There are at least two of them.

R.D.: OK. Here are the rules. Anything goes. You could have light coming out of your eyes to their hearts, your ears to their ears, any part of your body, or maybe there is a circle of light around you, a beam of light that comes down…

Woman: The problem is that in front of us there is a sort of fireworks.

R.D.: Us? You and that other person?

Woman: We are watching the fireworks, not knowing what to focus on.

R.D.: What kind of light is happening in you then? Is it fireworks in you, or is it just chaos of lights?

Woman: I don't think there is any light in us.

R.D.: There is no light in you at all.

Woman: But there is a light beyond the fireworks.

R.D.: So, we are standing with the fireworks here. There is no light in us at all. But there is a kind of light on the other side of the fireworks.

So, let's explore, What is the impasse?

— p. 153 —

Figure (p.153)

Is it a diffusion? Is it damping? Is it a loss of impetus? Is it a deviation? Is it a break in continuity? Is it an impossibility of transformation?

It seems to me there is a loss of impetus in you and the fireworks is a type of diffusion.

Woman: Yes, we definitely lack any impetus in the situation. And the fireworks go in every direction.

R.D.: Let's take our next step that is to find a situation that involves at least as wide and maybe wider solution space. Use the information you gathered from the impasse image to help you to know where to look. But it is not about the content of the problem or solution, it is about the system and this metaphor of light.

Woman: I think I have something like that.

R.D.: Alright, put yourself into that resourceful experience over in this other location and show it with your body. Maybe we'll see the fireworks coming from inside you now!

(Woman stands in the location. Her body is erect, eyes are up and she breathes deeply.)

Ok, fantastic, step back out. Now let's visualize this one. What does this look like?

Woman: In fact, I am alone and there is a huge light in my head, but it can light up all the space around me.

R.D.: (To audience) By the way, we are making a metaphor out here, but to me, what is really important to keep in mind is that this metaphor is connected to concrete experience. That is not just a picture. It is connected to something real. Therefore, it is a way of abstracting some kind of pattern or process from this experience, without getting caught up in the content. So, it is not like, "Well this is just a picture of light in your head." The light is an anchor, or a metaphor. It is connected to very specific intuitions.

— p. 152 —

Figure (p.152)

For instance, the man I mentioned earlier who has visualized the light damping in his product development team, found an interesting resource through this process. When he was describing his resource experience he said, "I send a out sort of a pink light. It isn't very bright. It is just a glow. And inside the people who are surrounded by the pink light, little red flames start to light up. And this red light starts to come brighter inside of them until they are all lit up." It was a kind of interesting difference.

(To the woman) Now we will apply this resource to that impasse, first metaphorically and then concretely. We first move from the concrete to the abstract – then back again from the abstract to concrete; from vision into action.

What if you can take this form of light from the positive reference experience and bring it into the impasse experience? How would that whole light painting begin to change?

Woman: Instead of having fireworks, it would be like lit up stars in my head.

R.D.: If you take the light in your head instead of focusing on the fireworks, there are stars. What about the person next to you?

Woman: It is very funny because that person becomes one star.

— p. 151 —

R.D.: This person, who was with you before, would become a star. There are a lot of beautiful things that can happen when we start to do this.

Figure (p.151)

What about the light on the other side? Woman: There is no light anymore.

R.D.: There is no light on the other side?

Woman: No, it is in me.

R.D.: Aha. Now we are going to step into this impasse again and feel what it means concretely. What does this light really mean in terms of your actual actions, language and behavior?

Step into this new painting.

Woman: (Steps into the impasse location and laughs) This is great!

R.D.: You don't have to have a specific plan just yet but think about what options you might have if you looked at the problem in this light. And step out again when you have some good ideas.

Woman: (Steps out of impasse location) What is nice from what I just lived, is that it is as if there was a house and stairs to the door. And now I am climbing the steps.

R.D.: Even the metaphor changes.

Woman: Thank you very much!

— p. 150 —

R.D.: (To audience) If we are going to start thinking globally and systemically we have to have ways of thinking that allow us to track many, many variables that are connected to each other. You need new ways of thinking if you hope to develop global thinking strategies that allow you to think systemically and actually see models of things that are interconnected and interdependent without getting confused. And to me, this is one of the most simple, and yet profound ways that I know to do that.

For instance, in the case of the woman who had the student with the black hole in him; when she finished the picture, she realized, "I first need to send a green light from my heart to his heart, and I can't do it from the position I always take with respect to him. I have to get my heart closer, and if I come around next to him and just slightly behind him, supporting him, I can stand between the blackness in his family, and send this green light to him. Then when I put in the white light, it has a completely different action."

Again, the image is beautiful but she could also translate that into a physical expression. The green light from the heart to the heart might end up being done through a voice tone rather than a light.

Figure (p.150)

A very focused white light might end up being explicit questions as you try to illuminate something. A shift from red to green might indicate looking for the positive intention behind something. In other words, you begin to speak a language of light to the unconscious; a language that doesn't need conscious, logical reasoning in order to function.

I've even been told this is similar to certain spiritual healing techniques that people use.

Now, I would like you all to shine like rainbows onto someone else's problem. Take a cup of shimmering golden light, and shine like a beam out to another person in the group and find some impasse to work with – an unfulfilled dream, a problem, it can even be in a relationship with someone that you work with. Do this process as an exercise. Try to represent the whole problem space. Be a light artist and paint what the situation at the impasse looks like. Then find a resourceful reference experience. Paint that one as light as well. Then transfer light pattern from the resource image to the painting of the impasse and step back into it.

— p. 149 —

There are a lot of pleasant surprises that can happen. If you let your unconscious intuitions help you with it, some fascinating things start "bubbling up."

After the Exercise

It is so bright in here! (Laughter) Any question, reports, comments about the exercise?

P 1: It changed a belief. My belief was that in what situation I had to fight, and in fact, I no longer have to fight. I dance!

R.D.: In some ways, I think that the language of light is a language that speaks more at a belief level or at a belief or identity level than at a behavior level. It is less about how specifically to do A, B or C than it is about attitude or approach or values. It might actually be a way of shifting beliefs even more easily than some kinds of verbal techniques.

P 2: For me putting light in that place changed completely the situation, and not only this one, but many others as well.

R.D.: Another way of thinking about this as well is on a neurological level. What are you actually doing physiologically when you visualize light? The act of visualizing brings in a few billion more brain cells in connection with the problem. It stimulates and activates a whole other kind of neural network.

In fact, sometimes when I visualize somebody in their problem state, it is as if I were to look in their brain. The thinking pattern, the energy state I imagine in their brain seems like a light that is localized all in one area. It is so intensely focused in that area that the rest of the brain is just dim. Whereas when you have them visualize in terms of light, you are flipping on a switch and using this other hemisphere. The problem can't stay the same. It would be like having one person trying to solve a problem or opening it up to a group of a hundred people. All of a sudden you have a whole lot more potential options – just like Moshe Feldenkrais.

Conclusion: Creating Choice Through Awareness

Moshe Feldenkrais' legacy invites us to reconsider not only how we move, but how we know, learn, and change. His genius did not lie merely in a set of techniques, but in a way of perceiving human beings as dynamic, self-organizing systems in which mind, body and environment are inseparably intertwined. By attending to the process—the "how" of experience—he revealed that transformation emerges not from force or correction, but from awareness, variation, and the restoration of choice.

At the heart of Feldenkrais' strategy is a profound respect for the intelligence inherent in every individual. Rather than imposing solutions, he cultivated conditions in which new possibilities could arise organically. His use of constructed imagery, systemic thinking, and iterative feedback reflects a deeply generative epistemology—one that aligns closely with the principles of NLP and extends them into the somatic domain. In this way, Feldenkrais did not simply work on the body; he worked through it as a gateway to the whole person.

The elegance of his problem-solving strategy lies in its universality. Whether applied to physical rehabilitation, learning, communication, or complex organizational challenges, the same fundamental process holds: form a working picture of the system, identify where flow is disrupted,

— p. 148 —

classify the nature of the interruption, and introduce variations that restore coherence and continuity. This iterative loop between perception, imagination, and action exemplifies the essence of modeling—continually refining one's understanding in response to feedback from the system itself.

Perhaps most importantly, Feldenkrais reminds us that true change is not about eliminating limitations, but about expanding the range of possibilities available within them. By increasing awareness—both cognitive and somatic—we gain access to new choices, and with choice comes freedom. In this sense, his work is not only therapeutic, but deeply empowering.

In the broader context of the Strategies of Genius, Feldenkrais stands as a bridge between the inner world of thought and the lived reality of embodied experience. He demonstrates that intelligence is not confined to the brain but is distributed throughout the entire system of the self. His work challenges us to think with our whole being, to sense more finely, and to act with greater coherence.

Ultimately, Feldenkrais' contribution is a reminder that the path to mastery—whether in movement, thinking, or living—is not a matter of striving harder, but of becoming more aware. And in that awareness, we rediscover the innate capacity of the human system to learn, adapt, and evolve in ways that are both efficient and profoundly creative.

— p. 147 —
